<script>
  function detectColorScheme(){
    var theme="light";
    var current_theme = localStorage.getItem("ar5iv_theme");
    if(current_theme){
      if(current_theme == "dark"){
        theme = "dark";
      } }
    else if(!window.matchMedia) { return false; }
    else if(window.matchMedia("(prefers-color-scheme: dark)").matches) {
      theme = "dark"; }
    if (theme=="dark") {
      document.documentElement.setAttribute("data-theme", "dark");
    } else {
      document.documentElement.setAttribute("data-theme", "light"); } }

  detectColorScheme();

  function toggleColorScheme(){
    var current_theme = localStorage.getItem("ar5iv_theme");
    if (current_theme) {
      if (current_theme == "light") {
        localStorage.setItem("ar5iv_theme", "dark"); }
      else {
        localStorage.setItem("ar5iv_theme", "light"); } }
    else {
        localStorage.setItem("ar5iv_theme", "dark"); }
    detectColorScheme(); }
</script>

<div id="abstract1" class="ltx_abstract"><h6 class="ltx_title ltx_title_abstract">Abstract</h6>
    
<p id="abstract1.1" class="ltx_p">We propose a theoretical framework that generalizes simple and fast algorithms for hierarchical agglomerative clustering to weighted graphs with both attractive and repulsive interactions between the nodes. This framework defines GASP, a Generalized Algorithm for Signed graph Partitioning<span id="footnote1" class="ltx_note ltx_role_footnote"><sup class="ltx_note_mark">1</sup><span class="ltx_note_outer"><span class="ltx_note_content"><sup class="ltx_note_mark">1</sup>
          <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_note">1</span>
          
          
          
          
          
          
          
        Code available at: <a target="_blank" href="https://github.com/abailoni/GASP" title="" class="ltx_ref ltx_url ltx_font_typewriter">https://github.com/abailoni/GASP</a></span></span></span>, and allows us to explore many combinations of different linkage criteria and cannot-link constraints.
We prove the equivalence of existing clustering methods to some of those combinations and introduce new algorithms for combinations that have not been studied before.
We study both theoretical and empirical properties of these combinations and prove that some of these define an ultrametric on the graph.
We conduct a systematic comparison of various instantiations of GASP on a large variety of both synthetic and existing signed clustering problems, in terms of accuracy but also efficiency and robustness to noise.
Lastly, we show that some of the algorithms included in our framework, when combined with the predictions from a CNN model, result in a simple bottom-up instance segmentation pipeline.
Going all the way from pixels to final segments with a simple procedure, we achieve state-of-the-art accuracy on the CREMI 2016 EM segmentation benchmark without requiring domain-specific superpixels.</p>
  
</div>

<figcaption class="ltx_caption ltx_centering"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure"><span id="S0.F1.7" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">Figure 1</span>: </span><span id="S0.F1.8" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold" style="font-size:90%;">(a)<span id="S0.F1.8.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_medium"> Some iterations of GASP on a graph with attractive (green) and repulsive (red) interactions. At each iteration, the yellow edge with highest weight is contracted (example with sum linkage criterion is shown). </span>(b)<span id="S0.F1.8.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_medium"> Linkage criteria used in this paper demonstrated on two small clusters (see definitions in Table <a href="#S0.T1" title="Table 1 ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a> below). </span>(c)<span id="S0.F1.8.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_medium"> Application of GASP to instance segmentation: we show raw data from the CREMI neuron-segmentation challenge and some predictions of our CNN model, where white pixels represent boundary evidence. </span>(d)<span id="S0.F1.8.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_medium">
Seemingly similar linkage criteria can result in very different clustering dynamics, as shown in this example: color coded sequence of merges from early (white) via late (brown) to never (black).
</span></span></figcaption>
</figure>
<figure id="S0.T1" class="ltx_table ltx_align_center">
<figcaption class="ltx_caption ltx_centering" style="font-size:80%;"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_table"><span id="S0.T1.9" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:113%;">Table 1</span>: </span><span id="S0.T1.10" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:113%;">Conceptual contribution: Properties of clustering algorithms included in the proposed GASP framework, given a linkage criterion, a type of graph (signed or unsigned) and the optional use of cannot-link constraints. New constrained hierarchical clustering algorithms (HCC) proposed in this paper are highlighted in yellow. For algorithms typeset in bold font we prove that they define an ultrametric on the graph (Eq. <a href="#S3.E3" title="Equation 3 ‣ Algorithms defining an ultrametric ‣ 3.3 GASP: New and existing algorithms ‣ 3 Generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3</span></a>). For algorithms in the green box we show that they are weight-shift invariant (Prop. <a href="#S3.Thmprop2" title="Proposition 3.2. ‣ Average, Single, and Complete Linkage ‣ 3.3 GASP: New and existing algorithms ‣ 3 Generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3.2</span></a>).

<span id="S0.T1.11.5.6.3.1" class="ltx_tabular ltx_align_middle">
<span id="S0.T1.11.5.6.3.1.1" class="ltx_tr">
<span id="S0.T1.11.5.6.3.1.1.1" class="ltx_td ltx_nopad_r ltx_align_center"><span id="S0.T1.11.5.6.3.1.1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">HC-Complete</span></span></span>
</span></span><span id="S0.T1.11.5.6.4" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:80%;"> </span><span id="S0.T1.11.5.6.5" class="ltx_text"></span><span id="S0.T1.11.5.6.6" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:80%;"> </span><span class="ltx_inline-block"><svg id="S0.T1.pic9" class="ltx_picture" height="1.38" overflow="visible" version="1.1" viewBox="0 0 1.38 1.38" width="1.38"><g transform="translate(0,1.38) matrix(1 0 0 -1 0 0)"></g></svg></span></th></tr>
</tfoot>
</table>
</figure>
<section id="S1" class="ltx_section">
<h2 class="ltx_title ltx_title_section"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">1 </span>Introduction</h2>

<div id="S1.p1" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S1.p1.1" class="ltx_p">In computer vision, the partitioning of weighted graphs has been successfully applied to tasks as diverse as image segmentation, object tracking and pose estimation.
Most graph clustering methods work with positive edge weights only, which can be interpreted as similarities or distances between the nodes. These methods require users to specify the desired numbers of clusters (as in spectral clustering) or a termination criterion (e.g. in iterated normalized cuts) or even to add a seed for each object (e.g. seeded watershed or random walker).</p>
</div>
<div id="S1.p2" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S1.p2.1" class="ltx_p">Other graph clustering methods work with so-called <em id="S1.p2.1.1" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">signed graphs</em>, which feature both positive and negative edge weights corresponding to attraction and repulsion between nodes. The advantage of signed graphs over unsigned graphs is that balancing attraction and repulsion allows us to obtain a clustering without defining additional parameters. A canonical formulation of the signed graph partitioning problem is the <em id="S1.p2.1.2" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">multicut</em> or <em id="S1.p2.1.3" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">correlation clustering</em> problem <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite"><a href="#bib.bib35" title="" class="ltx_ref">kappes2011globally</a>; <a href="#bib.bib15" title="" class="ltx_ref">chopra1991multiway</a></cite>. This problem is NP-hard, though many approximate solvers have been proposed <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite"><a href="#bib.bib47" title="" class="ltx_ref">lange2018combinatorial</a>; <a href="#bib.bib62" title="" class="ltx_ref">pape2017solving</a>; <a href="#bib.bib8" title="" class="ltx_ref">beier2016efficient</a>; <a href="#bib.bib77" title="" class="ltx_ref">yarkony2012fast</a></cite> together with greedy agglomerative clustering algorithms <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite"><a href="#bib.bib39" title="" class="ltx_ref">keuper2015efficient</a>; <a href="#bib.bib51" title="" class="ltx_ref">levinkov2017comparative</a>; <a href="#bib.bib75" title="" class="ltx_ref">wolf2018mutex</a>; <a href="#bib.bib36" title="" class="ltx_ref">kardoostsolving</a></cite>.
Agglomerative clustering algorithms for signed graphs have clear advantages: they are parameter-free and efficient. Despite the fact that a variety of these algorithms exist, no overarching study has so far been conducted to compare their robustness and efficiency or to provide guidelines for matching an algorithm to the partitioning problem at hand.</p>
</div>
<div id="S1.p3" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S1.p3.1" class="ltx_p">Our first contribution is a simple theoretical framework that generalizes over agglomerative algorithms for signed graphs by linking them to hierarchical clustering (HC) on unsigned graphs (Section <a href="#S3.SS2" title="3.2 The GASP algorithm ‣ 3 Generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3.2</span></a>). This framework defines an underlying basic algorithm and allows us to explore its combinations with different linkage criteria and <em id="S1.p3.1.1" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">cannot-link constraints</em> (see Fig. <a href="#S0.F1" title="Figure 1 ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_ref ltx_nolink"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></span>a</a>, <a href="#S0.F1" title="Figure 1 ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_ref ltx_nolink"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></span>b</a>, and Table <a href="#S0.T1" title="Table 1 ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a>).
As second contribution, in Section <a href="#S3.SS3" title="3.3 GASP: New and existing algorithms ‣ 3 Generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3.3</span></a>, we formally prove that some of the combinations correspond to existing clustering algorithms, and introduce new algorithms for combinations which have not been explored before. By analyzing their theoretical properties, we also show that some of them define an ultrametric on the graph (see Table <a href="#S0.T1" title="Table 1 ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a>).</p>
</div>
<div id="S1.p4" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S1.p4.1" class="ltx_p">Third, we evaluate the algorithms on a large variety of both existing and synthetically generated signed graph clustering problems (Section <a href="#S4" title="4 Experiments ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">4</span></a>).
Fourth and finally, we also test the algorithms on <em id="S1.p4.1.1" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">instance segmentation</em> – a computer vision task consisting of assigning each pixel of an image to an object instance – by partitioning graphs whose edge weights are estimated by a CNN (see Fig. <a href="#S0.F1" title="Figure 1 ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_ref ltx_nolink"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></span>c</a> and Section <a href="#S4.SS2" title="4.2 Comparison of results and discussion ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">4.2</span></a>).
Our experiments show that the choice of linkage criterion markedly influences how clusters are grown by the agglomerative algorithms (Fig. <a href="#S0.F1" title="Figure 1 ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_ref ltx_nolink"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></span>d</a>), making some linkage methods more suited for certain types of clustering problems.
We benchmark the clustering algorithms by focusing on their efficiency, robustness and tendency to over- or under-cluster.
On instance segmentation, we show that the agglomerative algorithms outperform recently proposed spectral clustering methods, and that average-linkage based agglomerative algorithms achieve state of the art results on the CREMI 2016 challenge for neuron segmentation of 3D electron microscopy image volumes of brain tissue.

<span class="ltx_inline-block"><svg id="S1.p4.pic1" class="ltx_picture" height="1" overflow="visible" version="1.1" viewBox="0 0 1 1" width="1"><g style="--ltx-stroke-color:#00FF00;--ltx-fill-color:#00FF00;--ltx-fg-color:#00FF00;" color="#00FF00" fill="#00FF00" stroke="#00FF00" stroke-width="1.0pt" transform="translate(0,1) matrix(1 0 0 -1 0 0)"><path style="fill:none" d="M 2.77 11.92 L 2.59 11.92 C 4.12 11.92 5.36 10.68 5.36 9.15 L 5.36 -6.17 C 5.36 -7.7 6.6 -8.94 8.13 -8.94 L 5.57 -8.94 C 7.1 -8.94 8.34 -7.7 8.34 -6.17 L 8.34 -9.92 C 8.34 -8.39 7.1 -7.15 5.57 -7.15 L 0.98 -7.15 C -0.55 -7.15 -0.55 -7.15 0.98 -7.15 L 0.98 -7.15 C -0.55 -7.15 -0.55 -7.15 0.98 -7.15 L -2.77 -7.15 C -1.24 -7.15 0 -5.91 0 -4.38 L 0 9.15 C 0 10.68 1.24 11.92 2.77 11.92 Z"></path></g></svg></span></p>
</div>
</section>
<section id="S2" class="ltx_section">
<h2 class="ltx_title ltx_title_section"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">2 </span>Related work</h2>

<div id="S2.p1" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S2.p1.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="S2.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Proposal-free instance segmentation methods</span> adopt a bottom-up approach by directly grouping pixels into instances. In the last years, there has been a growing interest in such methods that do not involve object detection because, in certain types of data, object instances cannot be approximated by bounding boxes <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite"><a href="#bib.bib41" title="" class="ltx_ref">kirillov2017instancecut</a>; <a href="#bib.bib5" title="" class="ltx_ref">bai2017deep</a></cite>.
Some use metric learning to predict high-dimensional associative pixel embeddings that map pixels of the same instance close to each other <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite"><a href="#bib.bib49" title="" class="ltx_ref">lee2019learning</a>; <a href="#bib.bib23" title="" class="ltx_ref">fathi2017semantic</a>; <a href="#bib.bib60" title="" class="ltx_ref">newell2017associative</a>; <a href="#bib.bib21" title="" class="ltx_ref">de2017semantic</a></cite>
and then retrieve final instances by applying a clustering algorithm <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite"><a href="#bib.bib44" title="" class="ltx_ref">kong2018recurrentPix</a></cite>.
Other recent methods let the model predict the relative coordinates of the instance center <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite"><a href="#bib.bib59" title="" class="ltx_ref">neven2019instance</a>; <a href="#bib.bib13" title="" class="ltx_ref">cheng2019panopticdeeplab</a></cite> or, given a pixel <math id="S2.p1.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="(x,y)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>y</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">(x,y)</annotation></semantics></math>, they train a model to generate the mask of the instance located at <math id="S2.p1.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="(x,y)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>y</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">(x,y)</annotation></semantics></math> <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite"><a href="#bib.bib70" title="" class="ltx_ref">sofiiuk2019adaptis</a></cite>.</p>
</div>
<div id="S2.p2" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S2.p2.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="S2.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Edge detection</span> also experienced recent progress thanks to deep learning, both on natural images <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite"><a href="#bib.bib29" title="" class="ltx_ref">Gao_2019_ICCV</a>; <a href="#bib.bib55" title="" class="ltx_ref">liu2018affinity</a>; <a href="#bib.bib76" title="" class="ltx_ref">xie2015holistically</a>; <a href="#bib.bib43" title="" class="ltx_ref">kokkinos2015pushing</a></cite> and biological data <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite"><a href="#bib.bib50" title="" class="ltx_ref">lee2017superhuman</a>; <a href="#bib.bib69" title="" class="ltx_ref">schmidt2018cell</a>; <a href="#bib.bib57" title="" class="ltx_ref">meirovitch2016multi</a>; <a href="#bib.bib18" title="" class="ltx_ref">ciresan2012deep</a></cite>. In neuron segmentation for connectomics, a field of neuroscience we also address in our experiments, boundaries are converted to final instances with subsequent postprocessing and superpixel-merging:
some use a combinatorial framework <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite"><a href="#bib.bib10" title="" class="ltx_ref">beier2017multicut</a></cite>, others use loopy graphs <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite"><a href="#bib.bib37" title="" class="ltx_ref">kaynig2015large</a>; <a href="#bib.bib45" title="" class="ltx_ref">krasowski2015improving</a></cite> or trees <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite"><a href="#bib.bib57" title="" class="ltx_ref">meirovitch2016multi</a>; <a href="#bib.bib54" title="" class="ltx_ref">liu2016sshmt</a>; <a href="#bib.bib52" title="" class="ltx_ref">liu2014modular</a>; <a href="#bib.bib26" title="" class="ltx_ref">funke2015learning</a>; <a href="#bib.bib72" title="" class="ltx_ref">uzunbas2016efficient</a></cite> to represent the region merging hierarchy. Flood-filling networks <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite"><a href="#bib.bib32" title="" class="ltx_ref">januszewski2018high</a></cite> and MaskExtend <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite"><a href="#bib.bib57" title="" class="ltx_ref">meirovitch2016multi</a></cite> used a CNN to iteratively grow one region/neuron at the time.
A structured learning approach was also proposed in <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite"><a href="#bib.bib28" title="" class="ltx_ref">funke2018large</a>; <a href="#bib.bib71" title="" class="ltx_ref">turaga2009maximin</a></cite>.</p>
</div>
<div id="S2.p3" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S2.p3.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="S2.p3.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Agglomerative graph clustering</span> has often been applied to instance segmentation <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite"><a href="#bib.bib3" title="" class="ltx_ref">arbelaez2011contour</a>; <a href="#bib.bib66" title="" class="ltx_ref">ren2013image</a>; <a href="#bib.bib53" title="" class="ltx_ref">liu2016image</a>; <a href="#bib.bib68" title="" class="ltx_ref">salembier2000binary</a></cite> because of its efficiency as compared to other divisive approaches like graph cuts.
Novel termination criteria and merging strategies have often been proposed: the agglomeration in <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite"><a href="#bib.bib56" title="" class="ltx_ref">malmberg2011generalized</a></cite> deploys fixed sets of merge constraints;
the popular graph-based method <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite"><a href="#bib.bib24" title="" class="ltx_ref">felzenszwalb2004efficient</a></cite> stops the agglomeration when the merge costs exceed a measure of quality for the current clusters.
The optimization approach in <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite"><a href="#bib.bib40" title="" class="ltx_ref">kiran2014global</a></cite> performs greedy merge decisions that minimize a certain energy, while other pipelines use classical linkage criteria, e.g. average linkage <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite"><a href="#bib.bib55" title="" class="ltx_ref">liu2018affinity</a>; <a href="#bib.bib50" title="" class="ltx_ref">lee2017superhuman</a></cite>, median <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite"><a href="#bib.bib28" title="" class="ltx_ref">funke2018large</a></cite> or a linkage learned by a random forest classifier <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite"><a href="#bib.bib61" title="" class="ltx_ref">nunez2013machine</a>; <a href="#bib.bib42" title="" class="ltx_ref">knowles2016rhoananet</a></cite>.</p>
</div>
<div id="S2.p4" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S2.p4.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="S2.p4.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Clustering of signed graphs</span> has the goal of partitioning a graph with both attractive and repulsive cues. Finding an optimally balanced partitioning has a long history in combinatorial optimization <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite"><a href="#bib.bib30" title="" class="ltx_ref">grotschel1989cutting</a>; <a href="#bib.bib31" title="" class="ltx_ref">grotschel1990facets</a>; <a href="#bib.bib16" title="" class="ltx_ref">chopra1993partition</a></cite>. NP-hardness of the <em id="S2.p4.1.2" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">correlation clustering</em> problem was shown in <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite"><a href="#bib.bib7" title="" class="ltx_ref">bansal2004correlation</a></cite>, while the connection with graph multicuts was made by <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite"><a href="#bib.bib22" title="" class="ltx_ref">demaine2006correlation</a></cite>. Modern integer linear programming solvers can tackle problems of considerable size <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite"><a href="#bib.bib2" title="" class="ltx_ref">andres2012globally</a></cite>, but accurate approximations <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite"><a href="#bib.bib62" title="" class="ltx_ref">pape2017solving</a>; <a href="#bib.bib8" title="" class="ltx_ref">beier2016efficient</a>; <a href="#bib.bib77" title="" class="ltx_ref">yarkony2012fast</a></cite>, greedy agglomerative algorithms <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite"><a href="#bib.bib51" title="" class="ltx_ref">levinkov2017comparative</a>; <a href="#bib.bib74" title="" class="ltx_ref">wolf2019mutex</a>; <a href="#bib.bib39" title="" class="ltx_ref">keuper2015efficient</a>; <a href="#bib.bib36" title="" class="ltx_ref">kardoostsolving</a></cite> and persistence criteria <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite"><a href="#bib.bib48" title="" class="ltx_ref">lange2018partial</a>; <a href="#bib.bib47" title="" class="ltx_ref">lange2018combinatorial</a></cite> have been proposed for even larger graphs.
Another line of research is given by spectral clustering methods that, on the other hand, require the user to specify the number of clusters in advance. Recently, some of these methods have been generalized to graphs with signed weights <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite"><a href="#bib.bib19" title="" class="ltx_ref">Cucuringu2019SPONGEAG</a>; <a href="#bib.bib14" title="" class="ltx_ref">chiang2012scalable</a>; <a href="#bib.bib46" title="" class="ltx_ref">kunegis2010spectral</a></cite>, whereas others let the user specify must-link and cannot-link constraints between clusters <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite"><a href="#bib.bib65" title="" class="ltx_ref">rangapuram2012constrained</a>; <a href="#bib.bib73" title="" class="ltx_ref">wang2014constrained</a>; <a href="#bib.bib20" title="" class="ltx_ref">cucuringu2016simple</a></cite>.</p>
</div>
<div id="S2.p5" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S2.p5.1" class="ltx_p">This work reformulates the clustering algorithms of <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite"><a href="#bib.bib51" title="" class="ltx_ref">levinkov2017comparative</a>; <a href="#bib.bib75" title="" class="ltx_ref">wolf2018mutex</a>; <a href="#bib.bib39" title="" class="ltx_ref">keuper2015efficient</a></cite> in a generalized framework and adopts ideas from the proposal-free instance segmentation methods <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite"><a href="#bib.bib55" title="" class="ltx_ref">liu2018affinity</a>; <a href="#bib.bib75" title="" class="ltx_ref">wolf2018mutex</a>; <a href="#bib.bib50" title="" class="ltx_ref">lee2017superhuman</a></cite> to predict edge weights of a graph.</p>
</div>
</section>
<section id="S3" class="ltx_section">
<h2 class="ltx_title ltx_title_section"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">3 </span>Generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs</h2>

<section id="S3.SS1" class="ltx_subsection">
<h3 class="ltx_title ltx_title_subsection"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">3.1 </span>Notation</h3>

<div id="S3.SS1.p1" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S3.SS1.p1.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="S3.SS1.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Graph formalism</span> – We consider an undirected simple edge-weighted graph <math id="S3.SS1.p1.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathcal{G}(V,E,w^{+},w^{-})" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi class="ltx_font_mathcaligraphic">𝒢</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>V</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>E</mi><mo>,</mo><msup><mi>w</mi><mo>+</mo></msup><mo>,</mo><msup><mi>w</mi><mo>−</mo></msup><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathcal{G}(V,E,w^{+},w^{-})</annotation></semantics></math> with both attractive and repulsive edge attributes.
The weight function <math id="S3.SS1.p1.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="w^{+}:E\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{+}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><msup><mi>w</mi><mo>+</mo></msup><mo lspace="0.278em" rspace="0.278em">:</mo><mrow><mi>E</mi><mo stretchy="false">→</mo><msup><mi>ℝ</mi><mo>+</mo></msup></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">w^{+}:E\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{+}</annotation></semantics></math> associates to every edge a positive scalar attribute <math id="S3.SS1.p1.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="w_{e}^{+}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><msubsup><mi>w</mi><mi>e</mi><mo>+</mo></msubsup><mo>∈</mo><msup><mi>ℝ</mi><mo>+</mo></msup></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">w_{e}^{+}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}</annotation></semantics></math> representing a merge affinity or a similarity measure.
On the other hand, <math id="S3.SS1.p1.m4" class="ltx_Math" alttext="w^{-}:E\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{+}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><msup><mi>w</mi><mo>−</mo></msup><mo lspace="0.278em" rspace="0.278em">:</mo><mrow><mi>E</mi><mo stretchy="false">→</mo><msup><mi>ℝ</mi><mo>+</mo></msup></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">w^{-}:E\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{+}</annotation></semantics></math> associates to each edge a split tendency <math id="S3.SS1.p1.m5" class="ltx_Math" alttext="w_{e}^{-}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><msubsup><mi>w</mi><mi>e</mi><mo>−</mo></msubsup><mo>∈</mo><msup><mi>ℝ</mi><mo>+</mo></msup></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">w_{e}^{-}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}</annotation></semantics></math>.
Graphs of the type <math id="S3.SS1.p1.m6" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathcal{G}(V,E,w^{+},w^{-})" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi class="ltx_font_mathcaligraphic">𝒢</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>V</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>E</mi><mo>,</mo><msup><mi>w</mi><mo>+</mo></msup><mo>,</mo><msup><mi>w</mi><mo>−</mo></msup><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathcal{G}(V,E,w^{+},w^{-})</annotation></semantics></math> are often defined as <em id="S3.SS1.p1.1.2" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">signed graphs</em> <math id="S3.SS1.p1.m7" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathcal{G}(V,E,w)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi class="ltx_font_mathcaligraphic">𝒢</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>V</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>E</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>w</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathcal{G}(V,E,w)</annotation></semantics></math>, featuring positive and negative edge weights <math id="S3.SS1.p1.m8" class="ltx_Math" alttext="w_{e}\in\mathbb{R}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><msub><mi>w</mi><mi>e</mi></msub><mo>∈</mo><mi>ℝ</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">w_{e}\in\mathbb{R}</annotation></semantics></math>. Following the theoretical considerations in <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite"><a href="#bib.bib48" title="" class="ltx_ref">lange2018partial</a></cite>, we define signed weights as <math id="S3.SS1.p1.m9" class="ltx_Math" alttext="{w_{e}=w_{e}^{+}-w_{e}^{-}}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><msub><mi>w</mi><mi>e</mi></msub><mo>=</mo><mrow><msubsup><mi>w</mi><mi>e</mi><mo>+</mo></msubsup><mo>−</mo><msubsup><mi>w</mi><mi>e</mi><mo>−</mo></msubsup></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">{w_{e}=w_{e}^{+}-w_{e}^{-}}</annotation></semantics></math>.</p>
</div>
<div id="S3.SS1.p2" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S3.SS1.p2.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="S3.SS1.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Multicut objective</span> – We call the set <math id="S3.SS1.p2.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\Pi=\{S_{1},\ldots,S_{K}\}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">Π</mi><mo>=</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">{</mo><msub><mi>S</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">…</mi><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>S</mi><mi>K</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">}</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\Pi=\{S_{1},\ldots,S_{K}\}</annotation></semantics></math> a <em id="S3.SS1.p2.1.2" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">clustering</em> or <em id="S3.SS1.p2.1.3" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">partitioning</em> if <math id="S3.SS1.p2.m2" class="ltx_math_unparsed" alttext="V=\cup_{S\in\Pi}S" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>V</mi><mo rspace="0em">=</mo><mo lspace="0em">∪</mo><msub><mrow></mrow><mrow><mi>S</mi><mo>∈</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">Π</mi></mrow></msub><mi>S</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">V=\cup_{S\in\Pi}S</annotation></semantics></math>, <math id="S3.SS1.p2.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\,S\cap S^{\prime}=\emptyset" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mrow><mi>S</mi><mo>∩</mo><msup><mi>S</mi><mo>′</mo></msup></mrow><mo>=</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">∅</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\,S\cap S^{\prime}=\emptyset</annotation></semantics></math> for different clusters <math id="S3.SS1.p2.m4" class="ltx_Math" alttext="S,S^{\prime}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>S</mi><mo>,</mo><msup><mi>S</mi><mo>′</mo></msup></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">S,S^{\prime}</annotation></semantics></math> and every cluster <math id="S3.SS1.p2.m5" class="ltx_Math" alttext="S\in\Pi" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>S</mi><mo>∈</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">Π</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">S\in\Pi</annotation></semantics></math> induces a connected subgraph of <math id="S3.SS1.p2.m6" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathcal{G}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi class="ltx_font_mathcaligraphic">𝒢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathcal{G}</annotation></semantics></math>.
For any clustering <math id="S3.SS1.p2.m7" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\Pi" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi mathvariant="normal">Π</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\Pi</annotation></semantics></math> of <math id="S3.SS1.p2.m8" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathcal{G}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi class="ltx_font_mathcaligraphic">𝒢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathcal{G}</annotation></semantics></math>, we denote as <math id="S3.SS1.p2.m9" class="ltx_math_unparsed" alttext="E^{0}_{\Pi}=\{e_{uv}\in E\,|\,\exists S\in\Pi:u,v\in S\}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><msubsup><mi>E</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Π</mi><mn>0</mn></msubsup><mo>=</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">{</mo><msub><mi>e</mi><mrow><mi>u</mi><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>v</mi></mrow></msub><mo>∈</mo><mi>E</mi><mo fence="false" lspace="0.170em" rspace="0.337em" stretchy="false">|</mo><mo rspace="0.167em">∃</mo><mi>S</mi><mo>∈</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">Π</mi><mo lspace="0.278em" rspace="0.278em">:</mo><mi>u</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>v</mi><mo>∈</mo><mi>S</mi><mo stretchy="false">}</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">E^{0}_{\Pi}=\{e_{uv}\in E\,|\,\exists S\in\Pi:u,v\in S\}</annotation></semantics></math> the set of edges linking nodes in the same cluster. Its complementary set <math id="S3.SS1.p2.m10" class="ltx_Math" alttext="E_{\Pi}^{1}=E\setminus E^{0}_{\Pi}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><msubsup><mi>E</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Π</mi><mn>1</mn></msubsup><mo>=</mo><mrow><mi>E</mi><mo>∖</mo><msubsup><mi>E</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Π</mi><mn>0</mn></msubsup></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">E_{\Pi}^{1}=E\setminus E^{0}_{\Pi}</annotation></semantics></math> of edges linking nodes belonging to distinct clusters, is known as the <em id="S3.SS1.p2.1.4" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">multicut</em> of <math id="S3.SS1.p2.m11" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathcal{G}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi class="ltx_font_mathcaligraphic">𝒢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathcal{G}</annotation></semantics></math> associated to clustering <math id="S3.SS1.p2.m12" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\Pi" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi mathvariant="normal">Π</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\Pi</annotation></semantics></math>. The instance of the NP-hard <em id="S3.SS1.p2.1.5" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">minimum cost multicut problem</em> w.r.t. <math id="S3.SS1.p2.m13" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathcal{G}(V,E,w_{e})" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi class="ltx_font_mathcaligraphic">𝒢</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>V</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>E</mi><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>w</mi><mi>e</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathcal{G}(V,E,w_{e})</annotation></semantics></math> is the task of finding a clustering that optimally balances the attraction and repulsion in the graph and is given by the following binary integer program:</p>
<table id="S3.E1" class="ltx_equation ltx_eqn_table">

<tbody><tr class="ltx_equation ltx_eqn_row ltx_align_baseline">
<td class="ltx_eqn_cell ltx_eqn_center_padleft"></td>
<td class="ltx_eqn_cell ltx_align_center"><math id="S3.E1.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\min_{\Pi}\sum_{e\in E}w_{e}x_{e}^{\Pi},\qquad\text{where}\quad x^{\Pi}_{e}=\begin{cases}1&amp;\text{if }e\in E^{1}_{\Pi}\\
0&amp;\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}" display="block" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mrow><mi>min</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mrow><mmultiscripts><munder><mo movablelimits="false">∑</mo><mrow><mi>e</mi><mo>∈</mo><mi>E</mi></mrow></munder><mprescripts></mprescripts><mi mathvariant="normal">Π</mi><mrow></mrow></mmultiscripts><mo>⁡</mo><msub><mi>w</mi><mi>e</mi></msub></mrow><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><msubsup><mi>x</mi><mi>e</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Π</mi></msubsup></mrow></mrow><mo rspace="2.167em">,</mo><mtext>where</mtext><mspace style="width:1em;" width="1em"></mspace><mrow><msubsup><mi>x</mi><mi>e</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Π</mi></msubsup><mo>=</mo><mrow><mo>{</mo><mtable columnspacing="5pt" displaystyle="true" rowspacing="0pt"><mtr><mtd class="ltx_align_left" columnalign="left"><mn>1</mn></mtd><mtd class="ltx_align_left" columnalign="left"><mrow><mrow><mtext>if </mtext><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>e</mi></mrow><mo>∈</mo><msubsup><mi>E</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Π</mi><mn>1</mn></msubsup></mrow></mtd></mtr><mtr><mtd class="ltx_align_left" columnalign="left"><mn>0</mn></mtd><mtd class="ltx_align_left" columnalign="left"><mrow><mtext>otherwise</mtext><mo lspace="0em">.</mo></mrow></mtd></mtr></mtable></mrow></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\min_{\Pi}\sum_{e\in E}w_{e}x_{e}^{\Pi},\qquad\text{where}\quad x^{\Pi}_{e}=\begin{cases}1&amp;\text{if }e\in E^{1}_{\Pi}\\
0&amp;\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}</annotation></semantics></math></td>

</table>
</div>
<div id="S3.SS1.p3" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S3.SS1.p3.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="S3.SS1.p3.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Linkage criteria and hierarchical trees</span> –
Let the interaction <math id="S3.SS1.p3.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathcal{W}(S\cup S^{\prime})\in\mathbb{R}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mrow><mi class="ltx_font_mathcaligraphic">𝒲</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mi>S</mi><mo>∪</mo><msup><mi>S</mi><mo>′</mo></msup></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>∈</mo><mi>ℝ</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathcal{W}(S\cup S^{\prime})\in\mathbb{R}</annotation></semantics></math> between two clusters <math id="S3.SS1.p3.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="S,S^{\prime}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>S</mi><mo>,</mo><msup><mi>S</mi><mo>′</mo></msup></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">S,S^{\prime}</annotation></semantics></math> be defined as a function, named <em id="S3.SS1.p3.1.2" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">linkage criterion</em>, depending on the weights of <em id="S3.SS1.p3.1.3" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">all</em> edges connecting clusters <math id="S3.SS1.p3.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="S" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>S</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">S</annotation></semantics></math> and <math id="S3.SS1.p3.m4" class="ltx_Math" alttext="S^{\prime}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msup><mi>S</mi><mo>′</mo></msup><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">S^{\prime}</annotation></semantics></math>.
The linkage criteria tested in this article are listed and defined in Table <a href="#S0.T1" title="Table 1 ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a>.
A <em id="S3.SS1.p3.1.4" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">dendrogram</em> <math id="S3.SS1.p3.m5" class="ltx_Math" alttext="T" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>T</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">T</annotation></semantics></math> is a rooted binary tree<span id="footnote2" class="ltx_note ltx_role_footnote"><sup class="ltx_note_mark">2</sup><span class="ltx_note_outer"><span class="ltx_note_content"><sup class="ltx_note_mark">2</sup>
              <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_note">2</span>
              
              
              
              
              
              
              
            In general, one could look at trees that are not binary. However, the algorithms discussed in this paper always generate binary hierarchical trees, so nothing would be gained by this generalization.</span></span></span> representing the merging order of an agglomerative algorithm, such that the leaves of the tree are in one-to-one correspondence with <math id="S3.SS1.p3.m6" class="ltx_Math" alttext="V" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>V</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">V</annotation></semantics></math> and each node of the tree represents a merge between two clusters.
Let <math id="S3.SS1.p3.m7" class="ltx_Math" alttext="T_{\mathrm{R}},T_{\mathrm{L}}\subset T" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>T</mi><mi>R</mi></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>T</mi><mi>L</mi></msub></mrow><mo>⊂</mo><mi>T</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">T_{\mathrm{R}},T_{\mathrm{L}}\subset T</annotation></semantics></math> denote the subtrees rooted at the two children of the root node in <math id="S3.SS1.p3.m8" class="ltx_Math" alttext="T" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>T</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">T</annotation></semantics></math>.
For any two leaves <math id="S3.SS1.p3.m9" class="ltx_Math" alttext="u,v\in V" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mrow><mi>u</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>v</mi></mrow><mo>∈</mo><mi>V</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">u,v\in V</annotation></semantics></math>, let <math id="S3.SS1.p3.m10" class="ltx_Math" alttext="T[u\vee v]" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>T</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mrow><mi>u</mi><mo>∨</mo><mi>v</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">T[u\vee v]</annotation></semantics></math> be the subtree rooted at the least common ancestor <math id="S3.SS1.p3.m11" class="ltx_Math" alttext="(u\vee v)\in T" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mi>u</mi><mo>∨</mo><mi>v</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo>∈</mo><mi>T</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">(u\vee v)\in T</annotation></semantics></math> of nodes <math id="S3.SS1.p3.m12" class="ltx_Math" alttext="u" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>u</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">u</annotation></semantics></math> and <math id="S3.SS1.p3.m13" class="ltx_Math" alttext="v" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>v</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">v</annotation></semantics></math> (furthest from the root), and let <span id="S3.SS1.p3.1.5" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">leaves<math id="S3.SS1.p3.m14" class="ltx_Math" alttext="(T[u\vee v])\subseteq V" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mi>T</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mrow><mi>u</mi><mo>∨</mo><mi>v</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo>⊆</mo><mi>V</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">(T[u\vee v])\subseteq V</annotation></semantics></math></span> be the set of leaves of this subtree.
Given an agglomerative algorithm with merging tree <math id="S3.SS1.p3.m15" class="ltx_Math" alttext="T" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>T</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">T</annotation></semantics></math>, let <math id="S3.SS1.p3.m16" class="ltx_Math" alttext="h_{T}:V\times V\rightarrow\mathbb{N}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><msub><mi>h</mi><mi>T</mi></msub><mo lspace="0.278em" rspace="0.278em">:</mo><mrow><mrow><mi>V</mi><mo lspace="0.222em" rspace="0.222em">×</mo><mi>V</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">→</mo><mi>ℕ</mi></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">h_{T}:V\times V\rightarrow\mathbb{N}</annotation></semantics></math> denote the <em id="S3.SS1.p3.1.6" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">dendrogram-height</em> of each <math id="S3.SS1.p3.m17" class="ltx_Math" alttext="(u\vee v)\in T" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mi>u</mi><mo>∨</mo><mi>v</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo>∈</mo><mi>T</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">(u\vee v)\in T</annotation></semantics></math>, which is defined as the iteration number at which nodes <math id="S3.SS1.p3.m18" class="ltx_Math" alttext="u,v\in V" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mrow><mi>u</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>v</mi></mrow><mo>∈</mo><mi>V</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">u,v\in V</annotation></semantics></math> were merged by the algorithm (see example in Fig. <a href="#S0.F1" title="Figure 1 ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a>a). We also define <math id="S3.SS1.p3.m19" class="ltx_math_unparsed" alttext="\mathcal{W}{}_{T}(u,v)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi class="ltx_font_mathcaligraphic">𝒲</mi><mmultiscripts><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>u</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>v</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mprescripts></mprescripts><mi>T</mi><mrow></mrow></mmultiscripts></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathcal{W}{}_{T}(u,v)</annotation></semantics></math> as the signed interaction <math id="S3.SS1.p3.m20" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathcal{W}{}(S\cup S^{\prime})" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi class="ltx_font_mathcaligraphic">𝒲</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mi>S</mi><mo>∪</mo><msup><mi>S</mi><mo>′</mo></msup></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathcal{W}{}(S\cup S^{\prime})</annotation></semantics></math> between the two clusters <math id="S3.SS1.p3.m21" class="ltx_Math" alttext="S,S^{\prime}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>S</mi><mo>,</mo><msup><mi>S</mi><mo>′</mo></msup></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">S,S^{\prime}</annotation></semantics></math> that were merged at iteration <math id="S3.SS1.p3.m22" class="ltx_Math" alttext="h_{T}(u,v)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><msub><mi>h</mi><mi>T</mi></msub><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>u</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>v</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">h_{T}(u,v)</annotation></semantics></math>:</p>
<table id="S3.E2" class="ltx_equation ltx_eqn_table">

<tbody><tr class="ltx_equation ltx_eqn_row ltx_align_baseline">
<td class="ltx_eqn_cell ltx_eqn_center_padleft"></td>

</section>
<section id="S3.SS2" class="ltx_subsection">
<h3 class="ltx_title ltx_title_subsection"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">3.2 </span>The GASP algorithm</h3>

<div id="S3.SS2.p1" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S3.SS2.p1.1" class="ltx_p">Our main contribution is a generalized agglomerative algorithm for signed graph partitioning (GASP) that generalizes hierarchical clustering (HC) to signed graphs.
The framework, defined in the following, encompasses several known and new agglomerative algorithms on display in Table <a href="#S0.T1" title="Table 1 ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a>, which are differentiated by the linkage criterion employed, similarly to HC.</p>
</div>
<div id="S3.SS2.p2" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S3.SS2.p2.1" class="ltx_p">In Algorithm <a href="#alg1" title="Algorithm 1 ‣ 3.2 The GASP algorithm ‣ 3 Generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a>, we provide simplified pseudo-code for the proposed GASP algorithm. GASP implements a bottom-up approach that starts by assigning each node to its own cluster and then iteratively merges pairs of adjacent clusters. The algorithm proceeds in three phases.</p>
</div>
<div id="S3.SS2.p3" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S3.SS2.p3.1" class="ltx_p">In phase one, GASP selects the pair of clusters with the highest absolute interaction <math id="S3.SS2.p3.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="|\mathcal{W}(S\cup S^{\prime})|" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">|</mo><mrow><mi class="ltx_font_mathcaligraphic">𝒲</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mi>S</mi><mo>∪</mo><msup><mi>S</mi><mo>′</mo></msup></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo stretchy="false">|</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|\mathcal{W}(S\cup S^{\prime})|</annotation></semantics></math>, so that the most attractive and the most repulsive pairs are analyzed first. If the interaction is repulsive and the algorithm option <em id="S3.SS2.p3.1.1" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">addCannotLinkConstraints</em> is <span id="S3.SS2.p3.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">True</span>, then the two clusters are constrained so that their members can never merge in subsequent steps of phase one. If the interaction is attractive, then the clusters are merged, provided that they were not previously constrained.
After each merge, the interaction between the merged cluster and its neighbors is updated according to one of the linkage criteria <math id="S3.SS2.p3.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathcal{W}(S\cup S^{\prime})" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi class="ltx_font_mathcaligraphic">𝒲</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mi>S</mi><mo>∪</mo><msup><mi>S</mi><mo>′</mo></msup></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathcal{W}(S\cup S^{\prime})</annotation></semantics></math> listed in Table <a href="#S0.T1" title="Table 1 ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a>. Phase one terminates when all the remaining clusters are either constrained or share repulsive interactions. Note that, on unsigned graphs, in phase one all nodes are merged into a single cluster and GASP is then equivalent to a standard hierarchical clustering algorithm.</p>
</div>
<div id="S3.SS2.p4" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S3.SS2.p4.1" class="ltx_p">Phase two: Now that the clusters have grown in size, the algorithm removes the constraints previously introduced in phase one and merges all the clusters that still share an attractive interaction, merging the most attractive one first<span id="footnote3" class="ltx_note ltx_role_footnote"><sup class="ltx_note_mark">3</sup><span class="ltx_note_outer"><span class="ltx_note_content"><sup class="ltx_note_mark">3</sup>
              <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_note">3</span>
              
              
              
              
              
              
              
            Note that in the version of GASP without <em id="footnote3.2" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">cannotLinkConstraints</em>, nothing happens in phase two because all remaining interactions are repulsive.</span></span></span>. The final clustering <math id="S3.SS2.p4.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\Pi^{*}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">Π</mi><mo>∗</mo></msup><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\Pi^{*}</annotation></semantics></math> returned by GASP is found at the end of phase two and it is then composed of clusters sharing only mutual repulsive interactions.</p>
</div>
<div id="S3.SS2.p5" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S3.SS2.p5.1" class="ltx_p">Finally, in phase three, the algorithm keeps merging all clusters until only a single one is left and then returns the hierarchical tree <math id="S3.SS2.p5.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="T^{*}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msup><mi>T</mi><mo>∗</mo></msup><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">T^{*}</annotation></semantics></math> representing the full sequence of merging steps.
The algorithm was implemented using a standard HC implementation with computational complexity <math id="S3.SS2.p5.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathcal{O}(N^{2}\log N)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi class="ltx_font_mathcaligraphic">𝒪</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><msup><mi>N</mi><mn>2</mn></msup><mo lspace="0.167em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mi>log</mi><mo lspace="0.167em">⁡</mo><mi>N</mi></mrow></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathcal{O}(N^{2}\log N)</annotation></semantics></math> (details left in Appendix
<a href="#S6.SS1" title="A6.1 Implementation and complexity of GASP ‣ A6 Appendix ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">A6.1</span></a>).</p>
</div>
<figure id="alg1" class="ltx_float ltx_float_algorithm ltx_framed ltx_framed_top">

<figcaption class="ltx_caption ltx_align_left" style="font-size:80%;"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_float"><span id="alg1.5" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Algorithm 1</span> </span> GASP</figcaption><div class="ltx_flex_figure">

display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mrow><mi style="--ltx-fg-color:#00FF00;" class="ltx_font_mathcaligraphic" mathcolor="#00FF00" mathsize="0.670em">𝒲</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo encoding="application/x-tex">\mathcal{W}{}(S\cup S^{\prime})&gt;0</annotation></semantics></math>]</span><span id="alg1.l8.7" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:80%;"> </span><span id="alg1.l8.8" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold" style="font-size:80%;">and</span><span id="alg1.l8.9" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:80%;"> </span><span id="alg1.l8.10" class="ltx_text" mathsize="0.670em">,</mo><msup><mi mathsize="0.670em">S</mi><mo mathsize="0.670em">′</mo></msup></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">S,S^{\prime}</annotation></semantics></math></span><span id="alg1.l8.11" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:80%;"> </span><span id="alg1.l8.12" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold" style="font-size:80%;">not</span><span id="alg1.l8.13" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:80%;"> constrained</span><span id="alg1.l8.14" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:120%;">]</span><span id="alg1.l8.15" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:80%;"> </span><span             <span id="alg1.l29.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold" style="font-size:80%;">return</span><span id="alg1.l29.4" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:80%;"> </span><math id="alg1.l29.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\Pi^{*}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msup><mi mathsize="0.800em" mathvariant="normal">Π</mi><mo mathsize="0.800em">∗</mo></msup><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\Pi^{*}</annotation></semantics></math><span id="alg1.l29.5" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:80%;">, </span><math id="alg1.l29.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="T^{*}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msup><mi mathsize="0.800em">T</mi><mo mathsize="0.800em">∗</mo></msup><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">T^{*}</annotation></semantics></math><span id="alg1.l29.6" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:80%;">
</span>
</div>
</div></div>
</figure>
</section>
<section id="S3.SS3" class="ltx_subsection">
<h3 class="ltx_title ltx_title_subsection"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">3.3 </span>GASP: New and existing algorithms</h3>

<div id="S3.SS3.p1" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S3.SS3.p1.1" class="ltx_p">In this paper we focus on five linkage methods (see columns of Table <a href="#S0.T1" title="Table 1 ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a>). Many more linkage criteria have been applied to unsigned graphs <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite"><a href="#bib.bib61" title="" class="ltx_ref">nunez2013machine</a>; <a href="#bib.bib24" title="" class="ltx_ref">felzenszwalb2004efficient</a>; <a href="#bib.bib28" title="" class="ltx_ref">funke2018large</a></cite>, involving median-based<span id="footnote4" class="ltx_note ltx_role_footnote"><sup class="ltx_note_mark">4</sup><span class="ltx_note_outer"><span class="ltx_note_content"><sup class="ltx_note_mark">4</sup>
              <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_note">4</span>
              
              
              
              
              
              
              
            Median linkage is also implemented in our library (see implementation details Appendix
<a href="#S6.SS1" title="A6.1 Implementation and complexity of GASP ‣ A6 Appendix ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">A6.1</span></a>).
</span></span></span> or size-regularized methods, but we decided to focus this paper on those five criteria because they represent the most popular choices.</p>
</div>
<section id="S3.SS3.SSS0.Px1" class="ltx_paragraph">
<h4 class="ltx_title ltx_title_paragraph">Sum Linkage</h4>

<div id="S3.SS3.SSS0.Px1.p1" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S3.SS3.SSS0.Px1.p1.1" class="ltx_p">–
On signed graphs, the sum of two attractive (or repulsive) interactions is still attractive (repulsive). On the other hand, on unsigned graphs, a strong attractive interaction could be obtained by summing many weak interactions, which depending on the application could be undesirable. This explains why, to our knowledge, an agglomerative algorithm with sum linkage has never been used on unsigned graphs. On signed graphs, such an algorithm was pioneered in <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite"><a href="#bib.bib51" title="" class="ltx_ref">levinkov2017comparative</a>; <a href="#bib.bib39" title="" class="ltx_ref">keuper2015efficient</a></cite> and was named Greedy Agglomerative Edge Contraction (GAEC)<span id="footnote5" class="ltx_note ltx_role_footnote"><sup class="ltx_note_mark">5</sup><span class="ltx_note_outer"><span class="ltx_note_content"><sup class="ltx_note_mark">5</sup>
                <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_note">5</span>
                
                
                
                
                
                
                
              An algorithm equivalent to GAEC was recently independently re-proposed in <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite"><a href="#bib.bib12" title="" class="ltx_ref">chehreghani2020hierarchical</a></cite>.</span></span></span>.
GAEC always makes the <em id="S3.SS3.SSS0.Px1.p1.1.1" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">greedy choice</em> that most decreases the multicut objective defined in Eq. <a href="#S3.E1" title="Equation 1 ‣ 3.1 Notation ‣ 3 Generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a> each time two clusters with positive interaction are merged<span id="footnote6" class="ltx_note ltx_role_footnote"><sup class="ltx_note_mark">6</sup><span class="ltx_note_outer"><span class="ltx_note_content"><sup class="ltx_note_mark">6</sup>
                <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_note">6</span>
                
                
                
                
                
                
                
              In general, GASP cannot be seen as a local search algorithm of the multicut problem (for details see Appendix
<a href="#S6.SS2" title="A6.2 GASP relation to the multicut objective ‣ A6 Appendix ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">A6.2</span></a>).
</span></span></span>. The authors of <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite"><a href="#bib.bib51" title="" class="ltx_ref">levinkov2017comparative</a></cite> propose an algorithm named <em id="S3.SS3.SSS0.Px1.p1.1.2" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">GreedyFixation</em>, which is equivalent to phase one of GASP using cannot-link-constraints and a sum linkage. However, running both phase one and two of GASP with sum linkage (algorithm named HCC-Sum in this paper) performed better than GreedyFixation in our experiments.</p>
</div>
</section>
<section id="S3.SS3.SSS0.Px2" class="ltx_paragraph">
<h4 class="ltx_title ltx_title_paragraph">AbsMax Linkage</h4>

<div id="S3.SS3.SSS0.Px2.p1" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S3.SS3.SSS0.Px2.p1.1" class="ltx_p">– This linkage method is also specific to signed graphs, since on unsigned graphs it would be equivalent to single linkage. Here, we prove that the Mutex Watershed Algorithm <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite"><a href="#bib.bib75" title="" class="ltx_ref">wolf2018mutex</a></cite> can be seen as an agglomerative algorithm with AbsMax linkage (proofs of the following three propositions are given in Appendix
<a href="#S6.SS3" title="A6.3 Proofs of Propositions , , , and ‣ A6 Appendix ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">A6.3</span></a>):</p>
</div>
<div id="S3.Thmprop1" class="ltx_theorem ltx_theorem_prop">
<h6 class="ltx_title ltx_runin ltx_title_theorem"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_theorem"><span id="S3.Thmprop1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Proposition 3.1</span></span><span id="S3.Thmprop1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">.</span></h6>
<div id="S3.Thmprop1.p1" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S3.Thmprop1.p1.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="S3.Thmprop1.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">The GASP Algorithm <a href="#alg1" title="Algorithm 1 ‣ 3.2 The GASP algorithm ‣ 3 Generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a> with AbsMax linkage, with or without cannot link constraints, returns the same final clustering <math id="S3.Thmprop1.p1.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\Pi^{*}_{\mathrm{AbsMax}}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msubsup><mi mathvariant="normal">Π</mi><mi>AbsMax</mi><mo>∗</mo></msubsup><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\Pi^{*}_{\mathrm{AbsMax}}</annotation></semantics></math> also returned by the Mutex Watershed Algorithm (MWS) <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite"><a href="#bib.bib75" title="" class="ltx_ref">wolf2018mutex</a></cite>, which has empirical complexity <math id="S3.Thmprop1.p1.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathcal{O}(N\log N)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi class="ltx_font_mathcaligraphic">𝒪</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mi>N</mi><mo lspace="0.167em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mi>log</mi><mo lspace="0.167em">⁡</mo><mi>N</mi></mrow></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathcal{O}(N\log N)</annotation></semantics></math>.</span></p>
</div>
</div>
</section>
<section id="S3.SS3.SSS0.Px3" class="ltx_paragraph">
<h4 class="ltx_title ltx_title_paragraph">Average, Single, and Complete Linkage</h4>

<div id="S3.SS3.SSS0.Px3.p1" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S3.SS3.SSS0.Px3.p1.1" class="ltx_p">– These three linkage criteria have been thoroughly studied on unsigned graphs, but never - until very recently - on signed graphs. In concurrent independent related work <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite"><a href="#bib.bib12" title="" class="ltx_ref">chehreghani2020hierarchical</a></cite>, the authors prove that applying these three linkage methods to a signed graph is equivalent to applying them to the unsigned graph obtained by shifting all edge weights by a constant. Here, we prove which of the algorithms studied here are “intrinsically signed” and do not have this invariance-property:</p>
</div>
<div id="S3.Thmprop2" class="ltx_theorem ltx_theorem_prop">
<h6 class="ltx_title ltx_runin ltx_title_theorem"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_theorem"><span id="S3.Thmprop2.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Proposition 3.2</span></span><span id="S3.Thmprop2.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">.</span></h6>
<div id="S3.Thmprop2.p1" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S3.Thmprop2.p1.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="S3.Thmprop2.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">We call an agglomerative algorithm “weight-shift invariant” if the dendrogram <math id="S3.Thmprop2.p1.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="T" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>T</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">T</annotation></semantics></math> returned by the algorithm is invariant w.r.t. a shift of all edge weights <math id="S3.Thmprop2.p1.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="w_{e}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msub><mi>w</mi><mi>e</mi></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">w_{e}</annotation></semantics></math> by a constant <math id="S3.Thmprop2.p1.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\alpha\in\mathbb{R}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>α</mi><mo>∈</mo><mi>ℝ</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\alpha\in\mathbb{R}</annotation></semantics></math>. Among the variations of GASP, only hierarchical clustering with Average (HC-Avg), Single (HC-Single), and Complete linkage (HC-Complete) are weight-shift-invariant (see green box in Table <a href="#S0.T1" title="Table 1 ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a>).</span></p>
</div>
</div>
<div id="S3.SS3.SSS0.Px3.p2" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">
<p id="S3.SS3.SSS0.Px3.p2.1" class="ltx_p">Although average and single linkage methods have been largely studied on unsigned graphs, to our knowledge, they have never been combined with cannot-link constraints on signed graphs<span id="footnote7" class="ltx_note ltx_role_footnote"><sup class="ltx_note_mark">7</sup><span class="ltx_note_outer"><span class="ltx_note_content"><sup class="ltx_note_mark">7</sup>
                <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_note">7</span>
                
                
                
                
                
                
                
              Note that Complete linkage methods return the same clustering whether constraints are enforced or not (proof in Lemma
<a href="#S6.Thmtheorem3" title="Lemma A6.3. ‣ A6.3 Proofs of Propositions , , , and ‣ A6 Appendix ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">A6.3</span></a>,
in Appendix).</span></span></span>, so we name these algorithms <em id="S3.SS3.SSS0.Px3.p2.1.1" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">HCC-Avg</em> and <em id="S3.SS3.SSS0.Px3.p2.1.2" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">HCC-Single</em>.</p>
</div>
</section>
<section id="S3.SS3.SSS0.Px4" class="ltx_paragraph">
<h4 class="ltx_title ltx_title_paragraph">Algorithms defining an ultrametric</h4>

<div id="S3.SS3.SSS0.Px4.p1" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S3.SS3.SSS0.Px4.p1.1" class="ltx_p">--
The connection between agglomerative algorithms and ultrametrics<span id="footnote8" class="ltx_note ltx_role_footnote"><sup class="ltx_note_mark">8</sup><span class="ltx_note_outer"><span class="ltx_note_content"><sup class="ltx_note_mark">8</sup>
                <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_note">8</span>
                
                
                
                
                
                
                
              A metric space <math id="footnote8.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="(X,d)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>X</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>d</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">(X,d)</annotation></semantics></math> is an <em id="footnote8.2" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">ultrametric</em> if, for every <math id="footnote8.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="x,y,z\in X" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>x</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>y</mi><mo>,</mo><mrow><mi>z</mi><mo>∈</mo><mi>X</mi></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">x,y,z\in X</annotation></semantics></math>, <math id="footnote8.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="d(x,y)\leq\max\{d(x,z),d(y,z)\}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mrow><mi>d</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>y</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>≤</mo><mrow><mi>max</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">{</mo><mrow><mi>d</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>z</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>,</mo><mrow><mi>d</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>y</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>z</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo stretchy="false">}</mo></mrow></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">d(x,y)\leq\max\{d(x,z),d(y,z)\}</annotation></semantics></math>.</span></span></span> is well known. Usually, ultrametrics are associated to strictly positive <em id="S3.SS3.SSS0.Px4.p1.1.1" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">similarity</em> or <em id="S3.SS3.SSS0.Px4.p1.1.2" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">dissimilarity</em> measures on a graph. In our framework, a trivial ultrametric is always given by the height <math id="S3.SS3.SSS0.Px4.p1.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="h_{T}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msub><mi>h</mi><mi>T</mi></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">h_{T}</annotation></semantics></math> of the dendrogram. However, for some of the GASP variations, we now define an ultrametric based on the edge weights and the signed interactions between clusters, generalizing what has been done for HC on unsigned graphs <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite"><a href="#bib.bib33" title="" class="ltx_ref">johnson1967hierarchical</a>; <a href="#bib.bib58" title="" class="ltx_ref">milligan1979ultrametric</a></cite>. To define this measure and prove its ultrametric property, we first map the signed interaction <math id="S3.SS3.SSS0.Px4.p1.m2" class="ltx_math_unparsed" alttext="\mathcal{W}{}_{T}{}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi class="ltx_font_mathcaligraphic">𝒲</mi><msub><mrow></mrow><mi>T</mi></msub></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathcal{W}{}_{T}{}</annotation></semantics></math> defined in Eq. <a href="#S3.E2" title="Equation 2 ‣ 3.1 Notation ‣ 3 Generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a> to positive “pseudo-distances” <math id="S3.SS3.SSS0.Px4.p1.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="d_{T}:V\times V\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{+}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><msub><mi>d</mi><mi>T</mi></msub><mo lspace="0.278em" rspace="0.278em">:</mo><mrow><mrow><mi>V</mi><mo lspace="0.222em" rspace="0.222em">×</mo><mi>V</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">→</mo><msup><mi>ℝ</mi><mo>+</mo></msup></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">d_{T}:V\times V\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{+}</annotation></semantics></math>:</p>
<table id="S3.EGx1" class="ltx_equationgroup ltx_eqn_align ltx_eqn_table">

<tbody id="S3.E3"><tr class="ltx_equation ltx_eqn_row ltx_align_baseline">
<td class="ltx_eqn_cell ltx_eqn_center_padleft"></td>
<td class="ltx_td ltx_align_right ltx_eqn_cell"><math id="S3.E3.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\displaystyle d_{T}(u,v)\equiv" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>d</mi><mi>T</mi></msub><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>u</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>v</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>≡</mo><mphantom></mphantom></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\displaystyle d_{T}(u,v)\equiv</annotation></semantics></math></td>
<td class="ltx_td ltx_align_left ltx_eqn_cell"><math id="S3.E3.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\displaystyle\begin{cases}0&amp;\text{if}\,\,u=v\\
M-\mathcal{W}{}_{T}(u,v)&amp;\text{if}\,\,u\neq v\end{cases}\quad\forall u,v\in V" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mrow><mo>{</mo><mtable columnspacing="5pt" rowspacing="0pt"><mtr><mtd class="ltx_align_left" columnalign="left"><mn>0</mn></mtd><mtd class="ltx_align_left" columnalign="left"><mrow><mrow><mtext>if</mtext><mo lspace="0.340em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>u</mi></mrow><mo>=</mo><mi>v</mi></mrow></mtd></mtr><mtr><mtd class="ltx_align_left" columnalign="left"><mrow><mi>M</mi><mo>−</mo><mi class="ltx_font_mathcaligraphic">𝒲</mi><msub><mrow></mrow><mi>T</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>u</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>v</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mtd><mtd class="ltx_align_left" columnalign="left"><mrow><mrow><mtext>if</mtext><mo lspace="0.340em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>u</mi></mrow><mo>≠</mo><mi>v</mi></mrow></mtd></mtr></mtable></mrow><mspace style="width:1.167em;" width="1.167em"></mspace><mrow><mo rspace="0.167em">∀</mo><mi>u</mi></mrow><mo>,</mo><mrow><mi>v</mi><mo>∈</mo><mi>V</mi></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\displaystyle\begin{cases}0&amp;\text{if}\,\,u=v\\
M-\mathcal{W}{}_{T}(u,v)&amp;\text{if}\,\,u\neq v\end{cases}\quad\forall u,v\in V</annotation></semantics></math></td>
<td class="ltx_eqn_cell ltx_eqn_center_padright"></td>
<td rowspan="1" class="ltx_eqn_cell ltx_eqn_eqno ltx_align_middle ltx_align_right"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_equation ltx_align_right">(3)</span></td></tr></tbody>
<tbody id="S3.E4"><tr class="ltx_equation ltx_eqn_row ltx_align_baseline">
<td class="ltx_eqn_cell ltx_eqn_center_padleft"></td>
<td class="ltx_td ltx_align_right ltx_eqn_cell"><math id="S3.E4.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\displaystyle\mathrm{where}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>where</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\displaystyle\mathrm{where}</annotation></semantics></math></td>
<td class="ltx_td ltx_align_left ltx_eqn_cell"><math id="S3.E4.m2" class="ltx_math_unparsed" alttext="\displaystyle\quad M\equiv\epsilon+\max_{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}\in V,\,u^{\prime}\neq v^{\prime}}\mathcal{W}{}_{T}(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>M</mi><mo>≡</mo><mi>ϵ</mi><mo>+</mo><munder><mi>max</mi><mrow><mrow><mrow><msup><mi>u</mi><mo>′</mo></msup><mo>,</mo><msup><mi>v</mi><mo>′</mo></msup></mrow><mo>∈</mo><mi>V</mi></mrow><mo rspace="0.337em">,</mo><mrow><msup><mi>u</mi><mo>′</mo></msup><mo>≠</mo><msup><mi>v</mi><mo>′</mo></msup></mrow></mrow></munder><mi class="ltx_font_mathcaligraphic">𝒲</mi><mmultiscripts><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msup><mi>u</mi><mo>′</mo></msup><mo>,</mo><msup><mi>v</mi><mo>′</mo></msup><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mprescripts></mprescripts><mi>T</mi><mrow></mrow></mmultiscripts></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\displaystyle\quad M\equiv\epsilon+\max_{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}\in V,\,u^{\prime}\neq v^{\prime}}\mathcal{W}{}_{T}(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})</annotation></semantics></math></td>
<td class="ltx_eqn_cell ltx_eqn_center_padright"></td>
<td rowspan="1" class="ltx_eqn_cell ltx_eqn_eqno ltx_align_middle ltx_align_right"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_equation ltx_align_right">(4)</span></td></tr></tbody>
</table>
<p id="S3.SS3.SSS0.Px4.p1.2" class="ltx_p">and where <math id="S3.SS3.SSS0.Px4.p1.m4" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\epsilon&gt;0" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>ϵ</mi><mo>&gt;</mo><mn>0</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\epsilon&gt;0</annotation></semantics></math>.
We then prove the following proposition:</p>
</div>
<div id="S3.Thmprop3" class="ltx_theorem ltx_theorem_prop">
<h6 class="ltx_title ltx_runin ltx_title_theorem"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_theorem"><span id="S3.Thmprop3.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Proposition 3.3</span></span><span id="S3.Thmprop3.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">.</span></h6>
<div id="S3.Thmprop3.p1" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S3.Thmprop3.p1.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="S3.Thmprop3.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">Among the algorithms included in the GASP framework (see Table <a href="#S0.T1" title="Table 1 ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a>), only Mutex Watershed and hierarchical clustering with Average (HC-Avg), Single (HC-Single) and Complete linkage (HC-Complete) define an ultrametric <math id="S3.Thmprop3.p1.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="(V,d_{T^{*}})" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>V</mi><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>d</mi><msup><mi>T</mi><mo>∗</mo></msup></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">(V,d_{T^{*}})</annotation></semantics></math>, where <math id="S3.Thmprop3.p1.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="d_{T^{*}}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msub><mi>d</mi><msup><mi>T</mi><mo>∗</mo></msup></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">d_{T^{*}}</annotation></semantics></math> is defined in Eq. <a href="#S3.E3" title="Equation 3 ‣ Algorithms defining an ultrametric ‣ 3.3 GASP: New and existing algorithms ‣ 3 Generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3</span></a> and <math id="S3.Thmprop3.p1.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="T^{*}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msup><mi>T</mi><mo>∗</mo></msup><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">T^{*}</annotation></semantics></math> is the tree returned by the GASP Algorithm <a href="#alg1" title="Algorithm 1 ‣ 3.2 The GASP algorithm ‣ 3 Generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a>.</span></p>
</div>
</div>
<div id="S3.SS3.SSS0.Px4.p2" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">
<p id="S3.SS3.SSS0.Px4.p2.1" class="ltx_p">In summary, in this section we have extended the family of HC algorithms <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite"><a href="#bib.bib33" title="" class="ltx_ref">johnson1967hierarchical</a>; <a href="#bib.bib58" title="" class="ltx_ref">milligan1979ultrametric</a></cite> with “weight-based ultrametrics” to signed graphs. Next, we move to their empirical evaluation.</p>
</div>

<td id="S3.T2.11.9.1" class="ltx_td ltx_align_left"><em id="S3.T2.11.9.1.1" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic" style="font-size:70%;">Fruit-Fly Level Global</em><span id="S3.T2.11.9.1.2" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:70%;"> </span><cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite"><a href="#bib.bib62" title="" class="ltx_ref">pape2017solving</a></cite></td>
