<section id="S4" class="ltx_section">
<h2 class="ltx_title ltx_title_section"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">4 </span>Experiments</h2>

<section id="S4.SS1" class="ltx_subsection">
<h3 class="ltx_title ltx_title_subsection"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">4.1 </span>Signed graph clustering problems</h3>

<div id="S4.SS1.p1" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S4.SS1.p1.1" class="ltx_p">We evaluate the agglomerative clustering algorithms included in our framework on a large collection of both synthetic and real-world graphs with very different structures. The size of the graphs ranges from a few hundred to hundreds of millions of edges. In this way, we will highlight the strengths and limitations of the different linkage criteria introduced in the last section.</p>
</div>
<div id="S4.SS1.p2" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S4.SS1.p2.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="S4.SS1.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Synthetic SSBM graphs</span> – We first consider synthetic graphs generated by a signed stochastic block model (SSBM). We use an Erdős-Rényi random graph model <math id="S4.SS1.p2.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathcal{G}(N,p)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi class="ltx_font_mathcaligraphic">𝒢</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>N</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>p</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathcal{G}(N,p)</annotation></semantics></math> with <math id="S4.SS1.p2.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="N" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>N</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">N</annotation></semantics></math> vertices and edge probability <math id="S4.SS1.p2.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="p" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>p</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">p</annotation></semantics></math>. Following the approach in <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite"><a href="#bib.bib19" title="" class="ltx_ref">Cucuringu2019SPONGEAG</a></cite>, we partitioned the graph into <math id="S4.SS1.p2.m4" class="ltx_Math" alttext="k" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>k</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">k</annotation></semantics></math> <em id="S4.SS1.p2.1.2" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">ground-truth</em> clusters, such that edges connecting vertices belonging to the same cluster (different clusters, respectively) have Gaussian distributed edge weights centered at <math id="S4.SS1.p2.m5" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mu=1" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>μ</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mu=1</annotation></semantics></math> (<math id="S4.SS1.p2.m6" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mu=-1" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>μ</mi><mo rspace="0em">=</mo><mrow><mo lspace="0em">−</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mu=-1</annotation></semantics></math>, respectively) and with standard deviation <math id="S4.SS1.p2.m7" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\sigma=0.1" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>σ</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0.1</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\sigma=0.1</annotation></semantics></math>. To model noise, the sign of the edge weights is flipped independently with probability <math id="S4.SS1.p2.m8" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\eta" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>η</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\eta</annotation></semantics></math>.</p>
</div>
<div id="S4.SS1.p3" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S4.SS1.p3.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="S4.SS1.p3.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Existing signed graphs</span> – We use clustering instances from the OpenGM benchmark <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite"><a href="#bib.bib34" title="" class="ltx_ref">kappes2013comparative</a></cite> as well as biomedical segmentation instances <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite"><a href="#bib.bib62" title="" class="ltx_ref">pape2017solving</a></cite>. The dataset <em id="S4.SS1.p3.1.2" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">Image Segmentation</em> contains planar region-adjacency-graphs (RAG) that are constructed from superpixel adjacencies of photographs. The <em id="S4.SS1.p3.1.3" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">Knott-3D</em> datasets contains 3D-RAGs arising from volume images acquired by electron microscopy (EM). The set <em id="S4.SS1.p3.1.4" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">Modularity Clustering</em> contains complete graphs constructed from clustering problems on small social networks. The <em id="S4.SS1.p3.1.5" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">Fruit-Fly</em> 3D-RAG instances were generated from volume image scans of fruit fly brain matter. Instances <em id="S4.SS1.p3.1.6" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">Level 1-4</em> are progressively simplified versions of the global problem obtained via block-wise domain decomposition <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite"><a href="#bib.bib62" title="" class="ltx_ref">pape2017solving</a></cite>.</p>
</div>
<div id="S4.SS1.p4" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S4.SS1.p4.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="S4.SS1.p4.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Grid-graphs from CNN predictions</span> – We also evaluate the clustering methods on the task of neuron segmentation in EM image volumes using training data from the CREMI 2016 EM Segmentation Challenge <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite"><a href="#bib.bib27" title="" class="ltx_ref">cremiChallenge</a></cite>.
We train a 3D U-Net <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite"><a href="#bib.bib67" title="" class="ltx_ref">ronneberger2015u</a>; <a href="#bib.bib17" title="" class="ltx_ref">cciccek20163d</a></cite> using the same architecture as <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite"><a href="#bib.bib28" title="" class="ltx_ref">funke2018large</a></cite> and predict long-and-short range affinities
as described in <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite"><a href="#bib.bib50" title="" class="ltx_ref">lee2017superhuman</a></cite>. The predicted affinities <math id="S4.SS1.p4.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="a_{e}\in[0,1]" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><msub><mi>a</mi><mi>e</mi></msub><mo>∈</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mrow><mn>0</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mo stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">a_{e}\in[0,1]</annotation></semantics></math>, which represent how likely it is for a pair of pixels to belong to the same neuron segment, are then mapped to signed edge weights <math id="S4.SS1.p4.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="w_{e}=a_{e}-0.5" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><msub><mi>w</mi><mi>e</mi></msub><mo>=</mo><mrow><msub><mi>a</mi><mi>e</mi></msub><mo>−</mo><mn>0.5</mn></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">w_{e}=a_{e}-0.5</annotation></semantics></math>, resulting in a 3D grid-graph having a node for each pixel/voxel of the image<span id="footnote9" class="ltx_note ltx_role_footnote"><sup class="ltx_note_mark">9</sup><span class="ltx_note_outer"><span class="ltx_note_content"><sup class="ltx_note_mark">9</sup>
              <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_note">9</span>
              
              
              
              
              
              
              
            To map affinities to signed weights, we also tested the <em id="footnote9.2" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">logarithmic mapping</em> proposed in <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite"><a href="#bib.bib25" title="" class="ltx_ref">finkel2008enforcing</a>; <a href="#bib.bib2" title="" class="ltx_ref">andres2012globally</a></cite>, but it performed worse in our experiments.</span></span></span>.
We divided the three CREMI training samples, consisting of <math id="S4.SS1.p4.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\sim" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mo>∼</mo><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\sim</annotation></semantics></math>196 million voxels each, into five sub-blocks for a total of 15 clustering problems (named <em id="S4.SS1.p4.1.2" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">CREMI-gridGraph</em> in Table <a href="#S3.T2" title="Table 2 ‣ Algorithms defining an ultrametric ‣ 3.3 GASP: New and existing algorithms ‣ 3 Generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a>). See Appendix
<a href="#S6.SS5" title="A6.5 Application to neuron segmentation ‣ A6 Appendix ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">A6.5</span></a>
for extended details about training, data augmentation, and how we remove tiny clusters left after running GASP on the <em id="S4.SS1.p4.1.3" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">CREMI-gridGraph</em> clustering problems.</p>
</div>
<div id="S4.SS1.p5" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S4.SS1.p5.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="S4.SS1.p5.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">3D-RAG from CNN-predictions</span> – Lastly, we use the predictions of our CNN model to generate three graph instances (one for each CREMI training sample, named <em id="S4.SS1.p5.1.2" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">CREMI-3D-RAG</em> in Table <a href="#S3.T2" title="Table 2 ‣ Algorithms defining an ultrametric ‣ 3.3 GASP: New and existing algorithms ‣ 3 Generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a>), which have very similar structure to the <em id="S4.SS1.p5.1.3" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">Knott-3D</em> and <em id="S4.SS1.p5.1.4" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">Fruit-Fly</em> instances. We obtain these problems by using a pipeline that is very common in neuron segmentation: a watershed algorithm generates superpixels and from those a 3D region-adjacency graph is built, where edge weights are given by the CNN predictions averaged over the boundaries of adjacent superpixels (details in Appendix
<a href="#S6.SS5" title="A6.5 Application to neuron segmentation ‣ A6 Appendix ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">A6.5</span></a>).</p>
</div>
<figure id="S4.T3" class="ltx_table">

<td id="S4.T3.st3.3.6.1" class="ltx_td ltx_align_left"><span id="S4.T3.st3.3.6.1.1" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:50%;">PNI CNN </span><cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite"><a href="#bib.bib50" title="" class="ltx_ref">lee2017superhuman</a></cite></td>
<td id="S4.T3.st3.3.6.2" class="ltx_td ltx_align_center"><span id="S4.T3.st3.3.6.2.1" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:50%;">☒</span></td>
<td id="S4.T3.st3.3.6.3" class="ltx_td ltx_align_center"><span id="S4.T3.st3.3.6.3.1" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:50%;">0.228</span></td>
<td id="S4.T3.st3.3.6.4" class="ltx_td ltx_align_center"><span id="S4.T3.st3.3.6.4.1" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:50%;">0.116</span></td>
<td id="S4.T3.st3.3.6.5" class="ltx_td ltx_align_center"><span id="S4.T3.st3.3.6.5.1" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:50%;">0.345</span></td>
<td id="S4.T3.st3.3.6.6" class="ltx_td ltx_align_center"><span id="S4.T3.st3.3.6.6.1" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:50%;">0.106</span></td></tr>
<tr id="S4.T3.st3.3.7" class="ltx_tr">
<td id="S4.T3.st3.3.7.1" class="ltx_td ltx_align_left"><span id="S4.T3.st3.3.7.1.1" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:50%;">LSI-Masks </span><cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite"><a href="#bib.bib6" title="" class="ltx_ref">bailoni2020proposal</a></cite></td>
<td id="S4.T3.st3.3.7.2" class="ltx_td ltx_align_center"><span id="S4.T3.st3.3.7.2.1" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:50%;">☐</span></td>

<td id="S4.T3.st3.3.7.6" class="ltx_td ltx_align_center"><span id="S4.T3.st3.3.7.6.1" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:50%;">0.107</span></td></tr>
<tr id="S4.T3.st3.3.8" class="ltx_tr">

<tr id="S4.T3.st3.3.9" class="ltx_tr">

<tr id="S4.T3.st3.3.10" class="ltx_tr">

<tr id="S4.T3.st3.3.11" class="ltx_tr">

</figure></div></div>
<figcaption class="ltx_caption ltx_centering" style="font-size:50%;"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_table"><span id="S4.T3.15" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:180%;">Table 3</span>: </span><span id="S4.T3.16" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold" style="font-size:180%;">Tables (a-b)<span id="S4.T3.16.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_medium">: Scores and run times of algorithms in the GASP framework on the <em id="S4.T3.16.1.1" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">CREMI-gridGraph</em> and <em id="S4.T3.16.1.2" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">CREMI-3D-RAG</em> clustering problems: average linkage methods achieved the best accuracy. CREMI-score is given by the geometric mean of (VOI-split + VOI-merge) and ARAND error (lower is better).</span></span></figcaption>

</section>
<section id="S4.SS2" class="ltx_subsection">
<h3 class="ltx_title ltx_title_subsection"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">4.2 </span>Comparison of results and discussion</h3>

<div id="S4.SS2.p1" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S4.SS2.p1.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="S4.SS2.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Multicut objective values</span> – In Table <a href="#S3.T2" title="Table 2 ‣ Algorithms defining an ultrametric ‣ 3.3 GASP: New and existing algorithms ‣ 3 Generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a>, we report the values of the multicut objective obtained for clustering with different GASP algorithms<span id="footnote10" class="ltx_note ltx_role_footnote"><sup class="ltx_note_mark">10</sup><span class="ltx_note_outer"><span class="ltx_note_content"><sup class="ltx_note_mark">10</sup>
              <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_note">10</span>
              
              
              
              
              
              
              
            Objective values achieved by Single and Complete linkage methods are much worse compared to other algorithms and are reported in Table
<a href="#S6.T5" title="Table A5 ‣ CREMI-3D-rag instances ‣ A6.5 Application to neuron segmentation ‣ A6 Appendix ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">A5</span></a>,
in Appendix.</span></span></span>. Although many heuristics were proposed to better optimize this objective <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite"><a href="#bib.bib8" title="" class="ltx_ref">beier2016efficient</a>; <a href="#bib.bib9" title="" class="ltx_ref">beier2014cut</a>; <a href="#bib.bib38" title="" class="ltx_ref">kernighan1970efficient</a></cite>, these methods are out of the scope of this paper, since they do not scale to the largest graph instances considered here. By looking at results in Table <a href="#S3.T2" title="Table 2 ‣ Algorithms defining an ultrametric ‣ 3.3 GASP: New and existing algorithms ‣ 3 Generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a>, we observe that GAEC almost always achieves the lowest objective values, expect in the <em id="S4.SS2.p1.1.2" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">CREMI-gridGraph</em> instances. Despite this, on graphs where a ground truth clustering is known, GAEC does not achieve the lowest ARAND errors (see Tables <a href="#S4.T3.st1" title="Table 3a) ‣ Table 3 ‣ 4.1 Signed graph clustering problems ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3a)</span></a> and <a href="#S4.T3.st2" title="Table 3b) ‣ Table 3 ‣ 4.1 Signed graph clustering problems ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3b)</span></a>).</p>
</div>
<div id="S4.SS2.p2" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S4.SS2.p2.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="S4.SS2.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Size of growing clusters: Sum vs Avg linkage</span> –
In all the studied clustering problems, we empirically observe that sum-linkage algorithms like GAEC grow clusters one after the other, as shown in Fig. <a href="#S0.F1" title="Figure 1 ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_ref ltx_nolink"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></span>d</a> and Fig. <a href="#S4.F3" title="Figure 3 ‣ 4.2 Comparison of results and discussion ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3</span></a> by the agglomeration order of GAEC<span id="footnote11" class="ltx_note ltx_role_footnote"><sup class="ltx_note_mark">11</sup><span class="ltx_note_outer"><span class="ltx_note_content"><sup class="ltx_note_mark">11</sup>
              <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_note">11</span>
              
              
              
              
              
              
              
            This <em id="footnote11.2" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">flooding agglomeration-strategy</em> of GAEC was also observed in <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite"><a href="#bib.bib36" title="" class="ltx_ref">kardoostsolving</a></cite>.</span></span></span>. This is intuitively explained by the following: initially, many of the most attractive edge weights have very similar values; when the two nodes <math id="S4.SS2.p2.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="u,v" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>u</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>v</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">u,v</annotation></semantics></math> with the highest attraction are merged, there is a high chance that they will have a common neighboring node <math id="S4.SS2.p2.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="t" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>t</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">t</annotation></semantics></math> belonging to the same cluster; thus, the interaction between the merged nodes <math id="S4.SS2.p2.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="uv" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>u</mi><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>v</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">uv</annotation></semantics></math> and <math id="S4.SS2.p2.m4" class="ltx_Math" alttext="t" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>t</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">t</annotation></semantics></math> is likely assigned to the highest priority, because it is given by the sum of two highly attractive edge weights. This will then start a “chain reaction” where only a single cluster is agglomerated at the time.
In the following, we will show how this unique <em id="S4.SS2.p2.1.2" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">flooding strategy</em> of the sum-linkage methods can be both an advantage or a disadvantage, depending on the type of clustering problem.</p>
</div>
<div id="S4.SS2.p3" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S4.SS2.p3.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="S4.SS2.p3.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Comparison to spectral clustering</span> –
The spectral clustering methods for signed graphs SPONGE<sub id="S4.SS2.p3.1.2" class="ltx_sub"><span id="S4.SS2.p3.1.2.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">sym</span></sub> and SPONGE proposed by <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite"><a href="#bib.bib19" title="" class="ltx_ref">Cucuringu2019SPONGEAG</a></cite> achieved state of the art performances on SSBM synthetic graphs. Their competitive performances are also confirmed by our experiments in Fig. <a href="#S4.F2" title="Figure 2 ‣ 4.2 Comparison of results and discussion ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a>.
However, these methods do not scale up to the large graph instances considered here and they also require the user to specify the true number of clusters in advance, which is not known for other graph instances tested in this paper. In Appendix, Table
<a href="#S6.T6" title="Table A6 ‣ A6.6 Adding structured noise to CNN predictions ‣ A6 Appendix ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">A6</span></a>,
we report the scores achieved by these methods on a much smaller sub-instance of the <em id="S4.SS2.p3.1.3" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">CREMI-gridGraph</em> problem: even when the true number of clusters is specified in advance for the spectral methods, they perform much worse than other GASP algorithms, with an accuracy penalty of almost 50%. For these reasons, we exclude them from our other comparison experiments.</p>
</div>
<div id="S4.SS2.p4" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S4.SS2.p4.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="S4.SS2.p4.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">GASP on synthetic SSBM graphs</span> –
GASP algorithms using cannotLinkConstraints are not expected to perform well on these graphs, because of the type of employed sign noise, so we focus our comparison only on the GAEC, HC-Avg and MWS algorithms (using Sum, Average, and AbsMax linkage methods, respectively).
Empirically, we observe that GAEC is the agglomerative algorithm performing best on SSBM graphs, on par with spectral method SPONGE<sub id="S4.SS2.p4.1.2" class="ltx_sub"><span id="S4.SS2.p4.1.2.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">sym</span></sub> (see Fig. <a href="#S4.F2" title="Figure 2 ‣ 4.2 Comparison of results and discussion ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a>). Given the simple properties of SSBM graphs, we can now give a detailed explanation of these empirical results.
In SSBM graphs, the number of edges <math id="S4.SS2.p4.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="E_{ij}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msub><mi>E</mi><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>j</mi></mrow></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">E_{ij}</annotation></semantics></math>
connecting two clusters <math id="S4.SS2.p4.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="S_{i},S_{j}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><msub><mi>S</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>S</mi><mi>j</mi></msub></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">S_{i},S_{j}</annotation></semantics></math> is proportional to the product <math id="S4.SS2.p4.m4" class="ltx_Math" alttext="|S_{i}|\cdot|S_{j}|" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">|</mo><msub><mi>S</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo rspace="0.055em" stretchy="false">|</mo></mrow><mo rspace="0.222em">⋅</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">|</mo><msub><mi>S</mi><mi>j</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">|</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|S_{i}|\cdot|S_{j}|</annotation></semantics></math> of cluster sizes.
With Sum or Avg linkage methods, due to the law of large numbers, the flipping noise is “averaged out” as soon as the set <math id="S4.SS2.p4.m5" class="ltx_Math" alttext="E_{ij}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msub><mi>E</mi><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>j</mi></mrow></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">E_{ij}</annotation></semantics></math> becomes larger and clusters grow in size.
On the other hand, when clusters are small, it can happen that, for few clusters, several of their edges in <math id="S4.SS2.p4.m6" class="ltx_Math" alttext="E_{ij}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msub><mi>E</mi><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>j</mi></mrow></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">E_{ij}</annotation></semantics></math> are flipped and the algorithm makes a mistake by merging two clusters belonging to different ground truth communities. From this observation, it follows that the <em id="S4.SS2.p4.1.3" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">flooding strategy</em> of the sum-linkage algorithm GAEC is a very good strategy on these types of graphs, because clusters are immediately grown in size (see dendrograms in Fig. <a href="#S4.F3" title="Figure 3 ‣ 4.2 Comparison of results and discussion ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3</span></a>). Average linkage method HC-Avg instead performs much worse on these graphs because it grows small equally-sized clusters and makes several wrong merge-decisions at the beginning.
Lastly, the MWS algorithm is not expected to perform well on these graphs because of the high sensitivity of the AbsMax linkage to flipping noise. In Proposition
<a href="#S6.Thmprop2" title="Proposition A6.2. ‣ A6.4 Mutex Watershed on SSBM graphs ‣ A6 Appendix ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">A6.2</span></a>
(see Appendix), we prove that, at every iteration, the MWS algorithm makes a mistake with at least probability <math id="S4.SS2.p4.m7" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\eta" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>η</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\eta</annotation></semantics></math>, independently on the sizes of the two clusters that are popped from priority queue.
In summary, for the SSBM, we can obtain a deep understanding of the dynamics induced by various linkage criteria, and find that GAEC gives highest accuracy by a large margin.</p>
</div>
<figure id="S4.F2" class="ltx_figure">
<div class="ltx_flex_figure">
<div class="ltx_flex_cell ltx_flex_size_2">

<figcaption class="ltx_caption ltx_centering"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure"><span id="S4.F2.sf1.3" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">a)</span> </span><math id="S4.F2.sf1.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="k=20" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi mathsize="0.900em">k</mi><mo mathsize="0.900em">=</mo><mn mathsize="0.900em">20</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">k=20</annotation></semantics></math><span id="S4.F2.sf1.4" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">, <math id="S4.F2.sf1.m4" class="ltx_Math" alttext="p=0.1" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>p</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0.1</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">p=0.1</annotation></semantics></math></span></figcaption>
</figure></div><div class="ltx_flex_cell ltx_flex_size_2">
<figure id="S4.F2.sf2" class="ltx_figure ltx_figure_panel"><object type="image/svg+xml" data="/html/1906.11713/assets/summary_SSBM_experiments_k50.svg" id="S4.F2.sf2.g1" class="ltx_graphics ltx_centering ltx_img_square" width="685" height="629"></object>
<figcaption class="ltx_caption ltx_centering"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure"><span id="S4.F2.sf2.3" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">b)</span> </span><math id="S4.F2.sf2.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="k=50" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi mathsize="0.900em">k</mi><mo mathsize="0.900em">=</mo><mn mathsize="0.900em">50</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">k=50</annotation></semantics></math><span id="S4.F2.sf2.4" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">, <math id="S4.F2.sf2.m4" class="ltx_Math" alttext="p=0.2" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>p</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0.2</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">p=0.2</annotation></semantics></math></span></figcaption>
</figure></div></div>
<figcaption class="ltx_caption"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure"><span id="S4.F2.3" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">Figure 2</span>: </span><span id="S4.F2.4" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">
ARAND errors (median values over 20 experiments, lower is better) on synthetic graphs generated with SSBM. We consider <math id="S4.F2.m4" class="ltx_Math" alttext="k" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>k</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">k</annotation></semantics></math> ground truth communities of random size. Graphs have <math id="S4.F2.m5" class="ltx_Math" alttext="N=10000" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>N</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>10000</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">N=10000</annotation></semantics></math> nodes; edges are randomly added with probability <math id="S4.F2.m6" class="ltx_Math" alttext="p" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>p</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">p</annotation></semantics></math>.
</span></figcaption>
</figure>
<div id="S4.SS2.p5" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S4.SS2.p5.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="S4.SS2.p5.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Scores on CREMI instance-segmentation</span> –
SSBM graphs are <em id="S4.SS2.p5.1.2" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">non-planar</em>, and every edge has the same probability to be present in the graph. On the other hand, the <em id="S4.SS2.p5.1.3" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">gridGraph</em> and <em id="S4.SS2.p5.1.4" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">3D-RAG</em> graphs of Table <a href="#S3.T2" title="Table 2 ‣ Algorithms defining an ultrametric ‣ 3.3 GASP: New and existing algorithms ‣ 3 Generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a> are sparse and have a very regular structure: regardless of whether a node represents a pixel or a superpixel, it will only have edge connections with its neighbors in the image (up to a certain hop distance).
Tables <a href="#S4.T3.st1" title="Table 3a) ‣ Table 3 ‣ 4.1 Signed graph clustering problems ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3a)</span></a>-<a href="#S4.T3.st2" title="Table 3b) ‣ Table 3 ‣ 4.1 Signed graph clustering problems ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3b)</span></a> show that average linkage methods (HC-Avg, HCC-Avg) strongly outperform other methods on <em id="S4.SS2.p5.1.5" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">CREMI-gridGraph</em> instances and also achieve the best scores on <em id="S4.SS2.p5.1.6" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">CREMI-3D-rag</em> graphs. Sum-based linkage methods (GAEC, HCC-Sum) have a two times higher ARAND error on grid-graphs and often return under-clustered segments (see failure cases in Fig. <a href="#S4.F4" title="Figure 4 ‣ 4.2 Comparison of results and discussion ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">4</span></a>). This suggests that the <em id="S4.SS2.p5.1.7" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">flooding strategy</em> observed previously in the sum-linkage methods does not work on grid-graphs, because in this setup edge weights are predicted by a CNN and noise is strongly spatially-correlated <span id="footnote12" class="ltx_note ltx_role_footnote"><sup class="ltx_note_mark">12</sup><span class="ltx_note_outer"><span class="ltx_note_content"><sup class="ltx_note_mark">12</sup>
              <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_note">12</span>
              
              
              
              
              
              
              
            This effect is not as strong on <em id="footnote12.2" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">3D-RAG</em> graphs, because edge weights are computed by averaging CNN predictions (and noise) over the boundaries of adjacent supervoxels.</span></span></span>.
To fully test this hypothesis, we conduct a set of experiments where the CNN predictions are perturbed by adding structured noise and simulating additional artifacts like ‘‘holes’’ in the boundary evidence<span id="footnote13" class="ltx_note ltx_role_footnote"><sup class="ltx_note_mark">13</sup><span class="ltx_note_outer"><span class="ltx_note_content"><sup class="ltx_note_mark">13</sup>
              <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_note">13</span>
              
              
              
              
              
              
              
            See Appendix
<a href="#S6.SS6" title="A6.6 Adding structured noise to CNN predictions ‣ A6 Appendix ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">A6.6</span></a>
for details about how we perturbed the <em id="footnote13.2" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">CREMI-gridGraph</em> problems by using Perlin noise <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite"><a href="#bib.bib64" title="" class="ltx_ref">perlin2001noise</a>; <a href="#bib.bib63" title="" class="ltx_ref">perlin1985image</a></cite>, which is one of the most common gradient noises used in procedural pattern generation.</span></span></span>.
The plot in Fig. <a href="#S4.F5" title="Figure 5 ‣ 4.2 Comparison of results and discussion ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">5</span></a> confirms that HC-Avg and HCC-Avg are very robust algorihtms on this data, followed by Sum-linkage algorithms and the Mutex Watershed algorithm (MWS). It is not a surprise that the AbsMax linkage used by MWS is not robust to this type of structured noise. However, the scores and runtimes in Table <a href="#S4.T3.st1" title="Table 3a) ‣ Table 3 ‣ 4.1 Signed graph clustering problems ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3a)</span></a> prove how MWS can achieve high accuracy with 70% lower runtime compared to HC-Avg.</p>
</div>
<div id="S4.SS2.p6" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S4.SS2.p6.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="S4.SS2.p6.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Complete and Single Linkage</span> – We use these two linkage methods as baselines to highlight the difficulty of the studied graph clustering problems listed in Table <a href="#S3.T2" title="Table 2 ‣ Algorithms defining an ultrametric ‣ 3.3 GASP: New and existing algorithms ‣ 3 Generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a>. Scores in Tables <a href="#S4.T3.st1" title="Table 3a) ‣ Table 3 ‣ 4.1 Signed graph clustering problems ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3a)</span></a>-<a href="#S4.T3.st2" title="Table 3b) ‣ Table 3 ‣ 4.1 Signed graph clustering problems ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3b)</span></a> show their poor performance: Single linkage hierarchical clustering (HC-Single), which here is equivalent to thresholding the edge weights at <math id="S4.SS2.p6.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="w_{e}=0" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><msub><mi>w</mi><mi>e</mi></msub><mo>=</mo><mn>0</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">w_{e}=0</annotation></semantics></math> and computing connected components in the graph, often returned few big under-segmented clusters. HC-Complete returned instead a lot of over-segmented clusters.</p>
</div>
<figure id="S4.F3" class="ltx_figure"><img src="/html/1906.11713/assets/figs/dendrograms/new_agglo_order.png" id="S4.F3.g1" class="ltx_graphics ltx_centering ltx_img_landscape" width="329" height="111" alt="Refer to caption">
<figcaption class="ltx_caption ltx_centering"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure"><span id="S4.F3.3" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">Figure 3</span>: </span><span id="S4.F3.4" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">Clustering dynamics and accuracy of GASP variations on stochastic block models. The dendrograms result from three versions of GASP on a synthetic graph generated with SSBM (<math id="S4.F3.m5" class="ltx_Math" alttext="250" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mn>250</mn><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">250</annotation></semantics></math> nodes, edge probability <math id="S4.F3.m6" class="ltx_Math" alttext="p=0.05" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>p</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0.05</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">p=0.05</annotation></semantics></math>, flipping probability <math id="S4.F3.m7" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\eta=0.1" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>η</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0.1</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\eta=0.1</annotation></semantics></math>). Red and blue colors show which of the two equal-sized ground-truth communities each node belongs to. At the top, dendrograms are truncated at the level of the final clustering <math id="S4.F3.m8" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\Pi^{*}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">Π</mi><mo>∗</mo></msup><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\Pi^{*}</annotation></semantics></math> returned by GASP. </span></figcaption>
</figure>
<div id="S4.SS2.p7" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S4.SS2.p7.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="S4.SS2.p7.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Results on CREMI challenge</span> –
Table <a href="#S4.T3.st3" title="Table 3c) ‣ Table 3 ‣ 4.1 Signed graph clustering problems ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3c)</span></a> shows that the HCC-Avg and HC-Avg clustering algorithms achieve state-of-the-art accuracy on the CREMI challenge, when combined with predictions of our CNN.
Most of the other entries (apart from <em id="S4.SS2.p7.1.2" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">LSI-Masks</em> <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite"><a href="#bib.bib6" title="" class="ltx_ref">bailoni2020proposal</a></cite>) employ super-pixels based post-processing pipelines and cluster 3D-region-adjacency graphs. As we show in Table  <a href="#S4.T3.st2" title="Table 3b) ‣ Table 3 ‣ 4.1 Signed graph clustering problems ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3b)</span></a>, using superpixels considerably reduces the size of the clustering problem and, consequently, the post-processing time.
However, our method operating directly on pixels (<em id="S4.SS2.p7.1.3" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">gridGraph + HCC-Avg</em>) achieves better performances than superpixel-based methods (<em id="S4.SS2.p7.1.4" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">3D-RAG + HCC-Avg</em>) and does not require the parameter tuning necessary to obtain good super-pixels, which is usually highly dataset dependent.
To scale up our method operating on pixels, we divided each test-volume into four sub-blocks, and then combined the resulting clusterings by running the algorithms again on the combined graph.
The method <em id="S4.SS2.p7.1.5" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">3D-RAG + LiftedMulticut</em> based on the lifted multicut approximation of <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite"><a href="#bib.bib10" title="" class="ltx_ref">beier2017multicut</a></cite> achieves the best scores overall, but it takes into account different information through the lifted edge weights that also depend on additional raw-data and shape information from highly engineered super-pixels.</p>
</div>
<figure id="S4.F4" class="ltx_figure"><img src="/html/1906.11713/assets/comparison_GAEC_MWS_Avg.png" id="S4.F4.g1" class="ltx_graphics ltx_centering ltx_img_landscape" width="329" height="113" alt="Refer to caption">
<figcaption class="ltx_caption ltx_centering"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure"><span id="S4.F4.4" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">Figure 4</span>: </span><span id="S4.F4.5" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">Failure cases of three versions of GASP applied to neuron segmentation. Only <em id="S4.F4.5.1" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">wrongly</em> segmented regions are highlighted in different warm colors. Red arrows point to wrongly split regions; yellow arrows point to false merge errors. HC-Avg returned the best segmentation. Data is 3D, hence the same color could be assigned to parts of segments that appear disconnected in 2D.
</span></figcaption>
</figure>
<figure id="S4.F5" class="ltx_figure"><object type="image/svg+xml" data="/html/1906.11713/assets/noise_plots_adapted-rand_1.svg" id="S4.F5.g1" class="ltx_graphics ltx_centering ltx_img_landscape" width="322" height="126"></object>
<figcaption class="ltx_caption ltx_centering"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure"><span id="S4.F5.4" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">Figure 5</span>: </span><span id="S4.F5.5" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">
ARAND errors (median values over 20 experiments, lower is better) on <em id="S4.F5.5.1" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">CREMI-gridGraph</em> clustering problems perturbed with structured noise. Average-linkage algorithms proved to be the most robust.
</span></figcaption>
</figure>
</section>
</section>
<section id="S5" class="ltx_section">
<h2 class="ltx_title ltx_title_section"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">5 </span>Conclusion</h2>

<div id="S5.p1" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S5.p1.1" class="ltx_p">We have presented a unifying framework for agglomerative clustering of graphs with both positive and negative edge weights. This framework allowed us to explore new combinations of constraints and linkage criteria and to perform a consistent evaluation of all algorithms in it.
We have then analyzed several theoretical and empirical properties of these algorithms. On instance segmentation, algorithms based on an average linkage criterion outperformed all the others: they proved to be simple and robust approaches to process short- and long-range predictions of a CNN.
On biological images, these simple average agglomeration algorithms achieve state-of-the-art results without requiring the user to spend much time tuning complex task-dependent pipelines based on super-pixels.</p>
</div>
</section>

<section id="S6" class="ltx_section">
<h2 class="ltx_title ltx_title_section"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">A6 </span>Appendix</h2>

<section id="S6.SS1" class="ltx_subsection">
<h3 class="ltx_title ltx_title_subsection"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">A6.1 </span>Implementation and complexity of GASP</h3>

<section id="S6.SS1.SSS0.Px1" class="ltx_paragraph">
<h4 class="ltx_title ltx_title_paragraph">Update rules</h4>

<div id="S6.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p1" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S6.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p1.1" class="ltx_p">During the agglomerative process, the interaction between adjacent clusters has to be properly updated and recomputed, as shown in Algorithm <a href="#alg1" title="Algorithm 1 ‣ 3.2 The GASP algorithm ‣ 3 Generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a>.
An efficient way of implementing these updates can be achieved by representing the agglomeration as a sequence of <em id="S6.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p1.1.1" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">edge contractions</em> in the graph. Given a graph <math id="S6.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p1.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathcal{G}(V,E,w)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi class="ltx_font_mathcaligraphic">𝒢</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>V</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>E</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>w</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathcal{G}(V,E,w)</annotation></semantics></math> and a clustering <math id="S6.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p1.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\Pi" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi mathvariant="normal">Π</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\Pi</annotation></semantics></math>, we define the associated <em id="S6.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p1.1.2" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">contracted graph</em> <math id="S6.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p1.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\tilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\Pi}(\tilde{V},\tilde{E},\tilde{w})" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><msub><mover accent="true"><mi class="ltx_font_mathcaligraphic">𝒢</mi><mo>~</mo></mover><mi mathvariant="normal">Π</mi></msub><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mover accent="true"><mi>V</mi><mo>~</mo></mover><mo>,</mo><mover accent="true"><mi>E</mi><mo>~</mo></mover><mo>,</mo><mover accent="true"><mi>w</mi><mo>~</mo></mover><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\tilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\Pi}(\tilde{V},\tilde{E},\tilde{w})</annotation></semantics></math>, such that there exists exactly one representative node <math id="S6.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p1.m4" class="ltx_Math" alttext="|\tilde{V}\cap S|=1" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">|</mo><mrow><mover accent="true"><mi>V</mi><mo>~</mo></mover><mo>∩</mo><mi>S</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">|</mo></mrow><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|\tilde{V}\cap S|=1</annotation></semantics></math> for every cluster <math id="S6.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p1.m5" class="ltx_Math" alttext="S\in\Pi" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>S</mi><mo>∈</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">Π</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">S\in\Pi</annotation></semantics></math> . Edges in <math id="S6.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p1.m6" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\tilde{E}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mover accent="true"><mi>E</mi><mo>~</mo></mover><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\tilde{E}</annotation></semantics></math> represent adjacency-relationships between clusters
and the signed edge weights <math id="S6.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p1.m7" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\tilde{w}_{e}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msub><mover accent="true"><mi>w</mi><mo>~</mo></mover><mi>e</mi></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\tilde{w}_{e}</annotation></semantics></math> are given by inter-cluster interactions <math id="S6.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p1.m8" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\tilde{w}(e_{uv})=\mathcal{W}_{S_{u}\cup S_{v}}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mrow><mover accent="true"><mi>w</mi><mo>~</mo></mover><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>e</mi><mrow><mi>u</mi><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>v</mi></mrow></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>=</mo><msub><mi class="ltx_font_mathcaligraphic">𝒲</mi><mrow><msub><mi>S</mi><mi>u</mi></msub><mo>∪</mo><msub><mi>S</mi><mi>v</mi></msub></mrow></msub></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\tilde{w}(e_{uv})=\mathcal{W}_{S_{u}\cup S_{v}}</annotation></semantics></math>, where <math id="S6.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p1.m9" class="ltx_Math" alttext="S_{u}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msub><mi>S</mi><mi>u</mi></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">S_{u}</annotation></semantics></math> denotes the clustering including node <math id="S6.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p1.m10" class="ltx_Math" alttext="u" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>u</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">u</annotation></semantics></math>.
For the linkage criteria tested in this article, when two clusters <math id="S6.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p1.m11" class="ltx_Math" alttext="S_{u}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msub><mi>S</mi><mi>u</mi></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">S_{u}</annotation></semantics></math> and <math id="S6.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p1.m12" class="ltx_Math" alttext="S_{v}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msub><mi>S</mi><mi>v</mi></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">S_{v}</annotation></semantics></math> are merged, the interactions between the new cluster <math id="S6.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p1.m13" class="ltx_Math" alttext="S_{u}\cup S_{v}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><msub><mi>S</mi><mi>u</mi></msub><mo>∪</mo><msub><mi>S</mi><mi>v</mi></msub></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">S_{u}\cup S_{v}</annotation></semantics></math> and each of its neighbors depend only on the previous interactions involving <math id="S6.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p1.m14" class="ltx_Math" alttext="S_{u}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msub><mi>S</mi><mi>u</mi></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">S_{u}</annotation></semantics></math> and <math id="S6.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p1.m15" class="ltx_Math" alttext="S_{v}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msub><mi>S</mi><mi>v</mi></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">S_{v}</annotation></semantics></math>. Thus, we can recompute these interactions by using an <em id="S6.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p1.1.3" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">update rule</em> <math id="S6.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p1.m16" class="ltx_Math" alttext="f" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>f</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">f</annotation></semantics></math> that does not involve any loop over the edges of the original graph <math id="S6.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p1.m17" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathcal{G}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi class="ltx_font_mathcaligraphic">𝒢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathcal{G}</annotation></semantics></math>:</p>
<table id="S6.EGx2" class="ltx_equationgroup ltx_eqn_align ltx_eqn_table">

<p id="S6.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p1.2" class="ltx_p">In Fig. <a href="#S6.F6" title="Figure A6 ‣ Implementation ‣ A6.1 Implementation and complexity of GASP ‣ A6 Appendix ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">A6</span></a> we show an example of edge contraction and in Table <a href="#S6.T4" title="Table A4 ‣ Implementation ‣ A6.1 Implementation and complexity of GASP ‣ A6 Appendix ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">A4</span></a> we list the update rules associated to the linkage criteria we introduced in Table <a href="#S0.T1" title="Table 1 ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a>.</p>

</section>
<section id="S6.SS1.SSS0.Px2" class="ltx_paragraph">
<h4 class="ltx_title ltx_title_paragraph">Implementation</h4>

<div id="S6.SS1.SSS0.Px2.p1" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S6.SS1.SSS0.Px2.p1.1" class="ltx_p">Our implementation of GASP is based on an union-find data structure and a heap allowing deletion of its elements.
In Phases 2 and 3, GASP is equivalent to a standard hierarchical agglomerative clustering algorithm with complexity <math id="S6.SS1.SSS0.Px2.p1.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathcal{O}(N^{2}\log N)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi class="ltx_font_mathcaligraphic">𝒪</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><msup><mi>N</mi><mn>2</mn></msup><mo lspace="0.167em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mi>log</mi><mo lspace="0.167em">⁡</mo><mi>N</mi></mrow></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathcal{O}(N^{2}\log N)</annotation></semantics></math>. In Algorithm <span class="ltx_ref ltx_missing_label ltx_ref_self"></span>, we show our implementation of phase 1, involving cannot-link constraints.
In phase 1, the algorithm starts with each node assigned to its own cluster and sorts all edges <math id="S6.SS1.SSS0.Px2.p1.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="e\in E" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>e</mi><mo>∈</mo><mi>E</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">e\in E</annotation></semantics></math> in a heap/priority queue (PQ) by their absolute weight <math id="S6.SS1.SSS0.Px2.p1.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="|w_{e}|=|w_{e}^{+}-w_{e}^{-}|" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">|</mo><msub><mi>w</mi><mi>e</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">|</mo></mrow><mo>=</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">|</mo><mrow><msubsup><mi>w</mi><mi>e</mi><mo>+</mo></msubsup><mo>−</mo><msubsup><mi>w</mi><mi>e</mi><mo>−</mo></msubsup></mrow><mo stretchy="false">|</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|w_{e}|=|w_{e}^{+}-w_{e}^{-}|</annotation></semantics></math> in descending order, so that the most attractive and the most repulsive interactions are processed first. It then iteratively pops one edge <math id="S6.SS1.SSS0.Px2.p1.m4" class="ltx_Math" alttext="e_{uv}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msub><mi>e</mi><mrow><mi>u</mi><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>v</mi></mrow></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">e_{uv}</annotation></semantics></math> from PQ and, depending on the priority <math id="S6.SS1.SSS0.Px2.p1.m5" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\tilde{w}_{uv}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msub><mover accent="true"><mi>w</mi><mo>~</mo></mover><mrow><mi>u</mi><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>v</mi></mrow></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\tilde{w}_{uv}</annotation></semantics></math>, does the following: in case of attractive interaction <math id="S6.SS1.SSS0.Px2.p1.m6" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\tilde{w}_{uv}&gt;0" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><msub><mover accent="true"><mi>w</mi><mo>~</mo></mover><mrow><mi>u</mi><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>v</mi></mrow></msub><mo>&gt;</mo><mn>0</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\tilde{w}_{uv}&gt;0</annotation></semantics></math>, provided that <math id="S6.SS1.SSS0.Px2.p1.m7" class="ltx_Math" alttext="e_{uv}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msub><mi>e</mi><mrow><mi>u</mi><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>v</mi></mrow></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">e_{uv}</annotation></semantics></math> was not flagged as a cannot-link constraint, merge the connected clusters, perform an edge contraction of <math id="S6.SS1.SSS0.Px2.p1.m8" class="ltx_Math" alttext="e_{uv}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msub><mi>e</mi><mrow><mi>u</mi><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>v</mi></mrow></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">e_{uv}</annotation></semantics></math> in <math id="S6.SS1.SSS0.Px2.p1.m9" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\tilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\Pi}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msub><mover accent="true"><mi class="ltx_font_mathcaligraphic">𝒢</mi><mo>~</mo></mover><mi mathvariant="normal">Π</mi></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\tilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\Pi}</annotation></semantics></math> and update the priorities of new double edges as explained in Fig. <a href="#S6.F6" title="Figure A6 ‣ Implementation ‣ A6.1 Implementation and complexity of GASP ‣ A6 Appendix ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">A6</span></a>.
If, on the other hand, the interaction is repulsive (<math id="S6.SS1.SSS0.Px2.p1.m10" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\tilde{w}_{uv}\leq 0" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><msub><mover accent="true"><mi>w</mi><mo>~</mo></mover><mrow><mi>u</mi><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>v</mi></mrow></msub><mo>≤</mo><mn>0</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\tilde{w}_{uv}\leq 0</annotation></semantics></math>) and the option <span id="S6.SS1.SSS0.Px2.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">addCannotLinkContraints</span> of Alg. <span class="ltx_ref ltx_missing_label ltx_ref_self"></span> is <span id="S6.SS1.SSS0.Px2.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">True</span>, then the edge <math id="S6.SS1.SSS0.Px2.p1.m11" class="ltx_Math" alttext="e_{uv}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msub><mi>e</mi><mrow><mi>u</mi><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>v</mi></mrow></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">e_{uv}</annotation></semantics></math> is flagged as cannot-link constraint.</p>
</div>

<tr id="S6.T4.3.3" class="ltx_tr">
<td id="S6.T4.3.3.1" class="ltx_td ltx_align_right ltx_border_r"><span id="S6.T4.3.3.1.1" class="ltx_text"></span><span id="S6.T4.3.3.1.2" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;"> <span id="S6.T4.3.3.1.2.1" class="ltx_text">
<span id="S6.T4.3.3.1.2.1.1" class="ltx_tabular ltx_align_middle">

<span id="S6.T4.3.3.1.2.1.1.1.1" class="ltx_td ltx_nopad_r ltx_align_right">Absolute</span></span>
<span id="S6.T4.3.3.1.2.1.1.2" class="ltx_tr">
<span id="S6.T4.3.3.1.2.1.1.2.1" class="ltx_td ltx_nopad_r ltx_align_right">Maximum:</span></span>
</span></span><span id="S6.T4.3.3.1.2.2" class="ltx_text"></span></span></td>
<th id="S6.T4.3.3.2" class="ltx_td ltx_align_left ltx_th ltx_th_column ltx_th_row"><span id="S6.T4.3.3.2.1" class="ltx_text"></span><span id="S6.T4.3.3.2.2" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">
<span id="S6.T4.3.3.2.2.1" class="ltx_tabular ltx_align_middle">
<span id="S6.T4.3.3.2.2.1.1" class="ltx_tr">
<span id="S6.T4.3.3.2.2.1.1.1" class="ltx_td ltx_nopad_r ltx_align_left"><math id="S6.T4.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="f(\tilde{w}_{1},\tilde{w}_{2})=\begin{cases}\tilde{w}_{1}&amp;\text{if}\,\,|\tilde{w}_{1}|&gt;|\tilde{w}_{2}|\\
\tilde{w}_{2}&amp;\text{otherwise}\end{cases}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mrow><mi>f</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mover accent="true"><mi>w</mi><mo mathsize="1.110em">~</mo></mover><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mover accent="true"><mi>w</mi><mo mathsize="1.110em">~</mo></mover><mn>2</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>=</mo><mrow><mo>{</mo><mtable columnspacing="5pt" rowspacing="0pt"><mtr><mtd class="ltx_align_left" columnalign="left"><msub><mover accent="true"><mi>w</mi><mo mathsize="1.110em">~</mo></mover><mn>1</mn></msub></mtd><mtd class="ltx_align_left" columnalign="left"><mrow><mrow><mtext>if</mtext><mo lspace="0.340em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">|</mo><msub><mover accent="true"><mi>w</mi><mo mathsize="1.110em">~</mo></mover><mn>1</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">|</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>&gt;</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">|</mo><msub><mover accent="true"><mi>w</mi><mo mathsize="1.110em">~</mo></mover><mn>2</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">|</mo></mrow></mrow></mtd></mtr><mtr><mtd class="ltx_align_left" columnalign="left"><msub><mover accent="true"><mi>w</mi><mo mathsize="1.110em">~</mo></mover><mn>2</mn></msub></mtd><mtd class="ltx_align_left" columnalign="left"><mtext>otherwise</mtext></mtd></mtr></mtable></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">f(\tilde{w}_{1},\tilde{w}_{2})=\begin{cases}\tilde{w}_{1}&amp;\text{if}\,\,|\tilde{w}_{1}|&gt;|\tilde{w}_{2}|\\
\tilde{w}_{2}&amp;\text{otherwise}\end{cases}</annotation></semantics></math></span></span>
</span></span><span id="S6.T4.3.3.2.3" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;"><span id="S6.T4.3.3.2.3.1" class="ltx_text"></span></span></th></tr>

</span></span><span id="S6.T4.3.4.2.3" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;"><span id="S6.T4.3.4.2.3.1" class="ltx_text"></span></span></th></tr>

<th id="S6.T4.3.6.2" class="ltx_td ltx_align_left ltx_th ltx_th_column ltx_th_row"><span id="S6.T4.3.6.2.1" class="ltx_text"></span><span id="S6.T4.3.6.2.2" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">

<span id="S6.T4.3.6.2.2.1.1" class="ltx_tr">
