</div>
<div id="S6.SS3.p13" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S6.SS3.p13.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="S6.SS3.p13.1.1" class="ltx_text">Next, we are left to prove the <math id="S6.SS3.p13.m1" class="ltx_math_unparsed" alttext="(\Rightarrow)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em" stretchy="false">⇒</mo><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">(\Rightarrow)</annotation></semantics></math> side of the <em id="S6.SS3.p13.1.1.1" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">if and only if</em> statement: if <math id="S6.SS3.p13.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="(V,d_{T})" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>V</mi><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>d</mi><mi>T</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">(V,d_{T})</annotation></semantics></math> is an ultrametric, then assumption <a href="#S6.E7" title="Equation 7 ‣ Proposition A6.1. ‣ A6.3 Proofs of Propositions , , , and ‣ A6 Appendix ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">7</span></a> holds.
To prove this statement, we first rephrase it in the following equivalent form: if assumption <a href="#S6.E7" title="Equation 7 ‣ Proposition A6.1. ‣ A6.3 Proofs of Propositions , , , and ‣ A6 Appendix ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">7</span></a> does not hold, then <math id="S6.SS3.p13.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="(V,d_{T})" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>V</mi><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>d</mi><mi>T</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">(V,d_{T})</annotation></semantics></math> is not an ultrametric and <a href="#S6.E12" title="Equation 12 ‣ Proof. ‣ A6.3 Proofs of Propositions , , , and ‣ A6 Appendix ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">12</span></a> does not hold. If we negate assumption <a href="#S6.E7" title="Equation 7 ‣ Proposition A6.1. ‣ A6.3 Proofs of Propositions , , , and ‣ A6 Appendix ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">7</span></a>, there must be at least three <math id="S6.SS3.p13.m4" class="ltx_Math" alttext="u,v,t\in V" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>u</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>v</mi><mo>,</mo><mrow><mi>t</mi><mo>∈</mo><mi>V</mi></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">u,v,t\in V</annotation></semantics></math> such that:</span></p>
<table id="S6.E17" class="ltx_equation ltx_eqn_table">

<tbody><tr class="ltx_equation ltx_eqn_row ltx_align_baseline">
<td class="ltx_eqn_cell ltx_eqn_center_padleft"></td>
<td class="ltx_eqn_cell ltx_align_center"><math id="S6.E17.m1" class="ltx_math_unparsed" alttext="h_{T}(u,v)&lt;h_{T}(u,t)\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{W}{}_{T}(u,v)&lt;\mathcal{W}{}_{T}(u,t)." display="block" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><msub><mi>h</mi><mi>T</mi></msub><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>u</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>v</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo>&lt;</mo><msub><mi>h</mi><mi>T</mi></msub><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>u</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>t</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mtext>and</mtext><mi class="ltx_font_mathcaligraphic">𝒲</mi><mmultiscripts><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>u</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>v</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mprescripts></mprescripts><mi>T</mi><mrow></mrow></mmultiscripts><mo>&lt;</mo><mi class="ltx_font_mathcaligraphic">𝒲</mi><mmultiscripts><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>u</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>t</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mprescripts></mprescripts><mi>T</mi><mrow></mrow></mmultiscripts><mo lspace="0em">.</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">h_{T}(u,v)&lt;h_{T}(u,t)\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{W}{}_{T}(u,v)&lt;\mathcal{W}{}_{T}(u,t).</annotation></semantics></math></td>
<td class="ltx_eqn_cell ltx_eqn_center_padright"></td>
<td rowspan="1" class="ltx_eqn_cell ltx_eqn_eqno ltx_align_middle ltx_align_right"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_equation ltx_align_right">(17)</span></td></tr></tbody>
</table>
<p id="S6.SS3.p13.2" class="ltx_p"><span id="S6.SS3.p13.2.1" class="ltx_text">The first condition, in words, is again assuming that the algorithm first merges nodes <math id="S6.SS3.p13.m5" class="ltx_Math" alttext="u" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>u</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">u</annotation></semantics></math> and <math id="S6.SS3.p13.m6" class="ltx_Math" alttext="v" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>v</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">v</annotation></semantics></math>, and later it also merges node <math id="S6.SS3.p13.m7" class="ltx_Math" alttext="t" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>t</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">t</annotation></semantics></math> with the cluster containing <math id="S6.SS3.p13.m8" class="ltx_Math" alttext="u" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>u</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">u</annotation></semantics></math> and <math id="S6.SS3.p13.m9" class="ltx_Math" alttext="v" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>v</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">v</annotation></semantics></math>. Thus, we can rephrase this assumption as:</span></p>
<table id="S6.E18" class="ltx_equation ltx_eqn_table">

<tbody><tr class="ltx_equation ltx_eqn_row ltx_align_baseline">
<td class="ltx_eqn_cell ltx_eqn_center_padleft"></td>
<td class="ltx_eqn_cell ltx_align_center"><math id="S6.E18.m1" class="ltx_math_unparsed" alttext="\mathcal{W}{}_{T}(u,v)&lt;\mathcal{W}{}_{T}(u,t)=\mathcal{W}{}_{T}(v,t)." display="block" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi class="ltx_font_mathcaligraphic">𝒲</mi><mmultiscripts><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>u</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>v</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mprescripts></mprescripts><mi>T</mi><mrow></mrow></mmultiscripts><mo>&lt;</mo><mi class="ltx_font_mathcaligraphic">𝒲</mi><mmultiscripts><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>u</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>t</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mprescripts></mprescripts><mi>T</mi><mrow></mrow></mmultiscripts><mo>=</mo><mi class="ltx_font_mathcaligraphic">𝒲</mi><mmultiscripts><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>v</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>t</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mprescripts></mprescripts><mi>T</mi><mrow></mrow></mmultiscripts><mo lspace="0em">.</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathcal{W}{}_{T}(u,v)&lt;\mathcal{W}{}_{T}(u,t)=\mathcal{W}{}_{T}(v,t).</annotation></semantics></math></td>
<td class="ltx_eqn_cell ltx_eqn_center_padright"></td>
<td rowspan="1" class="ltx_eqn_cell ltx_eqn_eqno ltx_align_middle ltx_align_right"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_equation ltx_align_right">(18)</span></td></tr></tbody>
</table>
<p id="S6.SS3.p13.3" class="ltx_p"><span id="S6.SS3.p13.3.1" class="ltx_text">From this, it follows that</span></p>
<table id="S6.E19" class="ltx_equation ltx_eqn_table">

<tbody><tr class="ltx_equation ltx_eqn_row ltx_align_baseline">
<td class="ltx_eqn_cell ltx_eqn_center_padleft"></td>
<td class="ltx_eqn_cell ltx_align_center"><math id="S6.E19.m1" class="ltx_math_unparsed" alttext="\mathcal{W}{}_{T}(u,v)&lt;\min\{\mathcal{W}{}_{T}(u,t),\mathcal{W}{}_{T}(v,t)\}," display="block" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi class="ltx_font_mathcaligraphic">𝒲</mi><mmultiscripts><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>u</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>v</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mprescripts></mprescripts><mi>T</mi><mrow></mrow></mmultiscripts><mo>&lt;</mo><mi>min</mi><mrow><mo stretchy="false">{</mo><mi class="ltx_font_mathcaligraphic">𝒲</mi><mmultiscripts><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>u</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>t</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mprescripts></mprescripts><mi>T</mi><mrow></mrow></mmultiscripts><mo>,</mo><mi class="ltx_font_mathcaligraphic">𝒲</mi><mmultiscripts><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>v</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>t</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mprescripts></mprescripts><mi>T</mi><mrow></mrow></mmultiscripts><mo stretchy="false">}</mo></mrow><mo>,</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathcal{W}{}_{T}(u,v)&lt;\min\{\mathcal{W}{}_{T}(u,t),\mathcal{W}{}_{T}(v,t)\},</annotation></semantics></math></td>
<td class="ltx_eqn_cell ltx_eqn_center_padright"></td>
<td rowspan="1" class="ltx_eqn_cell ltx_eqn_eqno ltx_align_middle ltx_align_right"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_equation ltx_align_right">(19)</span></td></tr></tbody>
</table>
<p id="S6.SS3.p13.4" class="ltx_p"><span id="S6.SS3.p13.4.1" class="ltx_text">which is exactly the negation of the ultrametric property <a href="#S6.E12" title="Equation 12 ‣ Proof. ‣ A6.3 Proofs of Propositions , , , and ‣ A6 Appendix ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">12</span></a>.
∎</span></p>
</div>
</div>
<div id="S6.SS3.p14" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S6.SS3.p14.1" class="ltx_p">See <a href="#S3.Thmprop3" title="Proposition 3.3. ‣ Algorithms defining an ultrametric ‣ 3.3 GASP: New and existing algorithms ‣ 3 Generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3.3</span></a></p>
</div>
<div id="S6.SS3.8" class="ltx_proof">
<h6 class="ltx_title ltx_runin ltx_font_italic ltx_title_proof">Proof.</h6>
<div id="S6.SS3.p15" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S6.SS3.p15.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="S6.SS3.p15.1.1" class="ltx_text">Thanks to Prop. <a href="#S6.Thmprop1" title="Proposition A6.1. ‣ A6.3 Proofs of Propositions , , , and ‣ A6 Appendix ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">A6.1</span></a>, we know that <math id="S6.SS3.p15.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="(V,d_{T^{*}})" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>V</mi><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>d</mi><msup><mi>T</mi><mo>∗</mo></msup></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">(V,d_{T^{*}})</annotation></semantics></math> is an ultrametric if and only if assumption <a href="#S6.E7" title="Equation 7 ‣ Proposition A6.1. ‣ A6.3 Proofs of Propositions , , , and ‣ A6 Appendix ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">7</span></a> holds. Thus, in the following, we will prove which variations of the GASP Algorithm <a href="#alg1" title="Algorithm 1 ‣ 3.2 The GASP algorithm ‣ 3 Generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a> satisfy assumption <a href="#S6.E7" title="Equation 7 ‣ Proposition A6.1. ‣ A6.3 Proofs of Propositions , , , and ‣ A6 Appendix ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">7</span></a>.
In other words, we need to prove in which cases GASP merges clusters according to a monotonously decreasing order of signed interactions <math id="S6.SS3.p15.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathcal{W}{}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi class="ltx_font_mathcaligraphic">𝒲</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathcal{W}{}</annotation></semantics></math>.</span></p>
</div>
<div id="S6.SS3.p16" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S6.SS3.p16.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="S6.SS3.p16.1.1" class="ltx_text">GASP puts clusters in a priority queue (Algorithm <a href="#alg1" title="Algorithm 1 ‣ 3.2 The GASP algorithm ‣ 3 Generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a>, lines 5 and 15) and merges them starting from those with the highest interaction (lines 9, 19, and 26). However, the priority queue is updated each time two clusters are merged (lines 10, 20, and 27). Thus, to ensure a monotonously decreasing merging order, updated interactions involving a merged cluster should always be lower or equal than previously existing interactions (<span id="S6.SS3.p16.1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">condition 1</span>):</span></p>
<table id="S6.EGx5" class="ltx_equationgroup ltx_eqn_align ltx_eqn_table">

<tbody id="S6.Ex2"><tr class="ltx_equation ltx_eqn_row ltx_align_baseline">
<td class="ltx_eqn_cell ltx_eqn_center_padleft"></td>
<td class="ltx_td ltx_align_right ltx_eqn_cell"></td>
<td class="ltx_td ltx_align_left ltx_eqn_cell"><math id="S6.Ex2.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\displaystyle\forall S_{i}\in\Pi\setminus\{S_{1},S_{2}\}," display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mrow><mrow><mo rspace="0.167em">∀</mo><msub><mi>S</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></mrow><mo>∈</mo><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">Π</mi><mo>∖</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">{</mo><msub><mi>S</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>S</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">}</mo></mrow></mrow></mrow><mo>,</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\displaystyle\forall S_{i}\in\Pi\setminus\{S_{1},S_{2}\},</annotation></semantics></math></td>
<td class="ltx_eqn_cell ltx_eqn_center_padright"></td></tr></tbody>
<tbody id="S6.E20"><tr class="ltx_equation ltx_eqn_row ltx_align_baseline">
<td class="ltx_eqn_cell ltx_eqn_center_padleft"></td>
<td class="ltx_td ltx_align_right ltx_eqn_cell"><math id="S6.E20.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\displaystyle\mathcal{W}{}(S_{1}\cup S_{2}\cup S_{i})" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi class="ltx_font_mathcaligraphic">𝒲</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><msub><mi>S</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>∪</mo><msub><mi>S</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><mo>∪</mo><msub><mi>S</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\displaystyle\mathcal{W}{}(S_{1}\cup S_{2}\cup S_{i})</annotation></semantics></math></td>
<td class="ltx_td ltx_align_left ltx_eqn_cell"><math id="S6.E20.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\displaystyle\leq\max\{\mathcal{W}{}(S_{1}\cup S_{i}),\mathcal{W}{}(S_{2}\cup S_{i})\}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mphantom></mphantom><mo>≤</mo><mrow><mi>max</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">{</mo><mrow><mi class="ltx_font_mathcaligraphic">𝒲</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><msub><mi>S</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>∪</mo><msub><mi>S</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>,</mo><mrow><mi class="ltx_font_mathcaligraphic">𝒲</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><msub><mi>S</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><mo>∪</mo><msub><mi>S</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo stretchy="false">}</mo></mrow></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\displaystyle\leq\max\{\mathcal{W}{}(S_{1}\cup S_{i}),\mathcal{W}{}(S_{2}\cup S_{i})\}</annotation></semantics></math></td>
<td class="ltx_eqn_cell ltx_eqn_center_padright"></td>
<td rowspan="1" class="ltx_eqn_cell ltx_eqn_eqno ltx_align_middle ltx_align_right"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_equation ltx_align_right">(20)</span></td></tr></tbody>
</table>
<p id="S6.SS3.p16.2" class="ltx_p"><span id="S6.SS3.p16.2.1" class="ltx_text">where <math id="S6.SS3.p16.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\Pi" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi mathvariant="normal">Π</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\Pi</annotation></semantics></math> is a clustering, <math id="S6.SS3.p16.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathcal{W}{}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi class="ltx_font_mathcaligraphic">𝒲</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathcal{W}{}</annotation></semantics></math> is a linkage criteria, and <math id="S6.SS3.p16.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="S_{1},S_{2}\in\Pi" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><msub><mi>S</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><mrow><msub><mi>S</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><mo>∈</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">Π</mi></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">S_{1},S_{2}\in\Pi</annotation></semantics></math> are two clusters merged by the algorithm at a given iteration. If this condition is true then, in the following iterations, GASP can only merge clusters with lower (or equal) interaction values.</span></p>
</div>
<div id="S6.SS3.p17" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S6.SS3.p17.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="S6.SS3.p17.1.1" class="ltx_text">We also note that, in phase 1, the algorithm skips interactions that are both positive and constraint (condition at line 8 in Algorithm <a href="#alg1" title="Algorithm 1 ‣ 3.2 The GASP algorithm ‣ 3 Generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a>) and merges them only later in phase 2 (line 19), when constraints are removed.
Clearly, whenever this happens, a decreasing merging order is no longer ensured.
Thus, on top of condition 1, we also have that no merging decisions should be “delayed” from phase 1 to phase 2 (<span id="S6.SS3.p17.1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">condition 2</span>).</span></p>
</div>
<div id="S6.SS3.p18" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S6.SS3.p18.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="S6.SS3.p18.1.1" class="ltx_text">Condition 1 always holds for Average, Single, Complete, and AbsMax linkage criteria, but not for a Sum linkage criteria, because the sum of two positive numbers <math id="S6.SS3.p18.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="a,b" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>a</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>b</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">a,b</annotation></semantics></math> is always higher than <math id="S6.SS3.p18.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\max\{a,b\}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>max</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">{</mo><mrow><mi>a</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>b</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">}</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\max\{a,b\}</annotation></semantics></math>. This is also demonstrated in the toy example of Fig. <a href="#S0.F1" title="Figure 1 ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_ref ltx_nolink"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></span>a</a>, proving that, in general, Sum-linkage algorithms like GAEC or HCC-Sum do not define an ultrametric on the graph.</span></p>
</div>
<div id="S6.SS3.p19" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S6.SS3.p19.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="S6.SS3.p19.1.1" class="ltx_text">Thanks to Lemma <a href="#S6.Thmtheorem3" title="Lemma A6.3. ‣ A6.3 Proofs of Propositions , , , and ‣ A6 Appendix ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">A6.3</span></a>, we have that condition 2 always holds for algorithms based on AbsMax and Complete linkage, proving that the Mutex Watershed and HC-Complete algorithms define an ultra-metric (whether or not cannot-link-constraints are enforced). On the other hand, condition 2 does not hold for other variations of GASP involving cannot-link-constraints (HCC-Sum, HCC-Avg, and HCC-Single), which do not then define an ultrametric.</span></p>
</div>
<div id="S6.SS3.p20" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S6.SS3.p20.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="S6.SS3.p20.1.1" class="ltx_text">Finally, the remaining not constrained versions of GASP (HC-Avg, HC-Single, and HC-Complete) satisfy both conditions, so they define an ultrametric, confirming the well-known results of related work in hierarchical clustering on unsigned graphs <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite"><a href="#bib.bib33" title="" class="ltx_ref">johnson1967hierarchical</a>; <a href="#bib.bib58" title="" class="ltx_ref">milligan1979ultrametric</a></cite>.</span></p>
</div>
<div id="S6.SS3.p21" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S6.SS3.p21.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="S6.SS3.p21.1.1" class="ltx_text">∎</span></p>
</div>
</div>
</section>
<section id="S6.SS4" class="ltx_subsection">
<h3 class="ltx_title ltx_title_subsection"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">A6.4 </span>Mutex Watershed on SSBM graphs</h3>

<div id="S6.Thmprop2" class="ltx_theorem ltx_theorem_prop">
<h6 class="ltx_title ltx_runin ltx_title_theorem"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_theorem"><span id="S6.Thmprop2.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Proposition A6.2</span></span><span id="S6.Thmprop2.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">.</span></h6>
<div id="S6.Thmprop2.p1" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S6.Thmprop2.p1.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="S6.Thmprop2.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">Consider a graph generated by an Erdős-Rényi signed stochastic block model (SSBM) as described in Section <a href="#S4.SS1" title="4.1 Signed graph clustering problems ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">4.1</span></a>, with <math id="S6.Thmprop2.p1.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="N" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>N</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">N</annotation></semantics></math> nodes, edges added with probability <math id="S6.Thmprop2.p1.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="p" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>p</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">p</annotation></semantics></math>, sign-flip probability <math id="S6.Thmprop2.p1.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\eta&lt;0.5" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>η</mi><mo>&lt;</mo><mn>0.5</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\eta&lt;0.5</annotation></semantics></math>, <math id="S6.Thmprop2.p1.m4" class="ltx_Math" alttext="k" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>k</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">k</annotation></semantics></math> ground-truth clusters, and edge weights Gaussian-distributed with standard deviation <math id="S6.Thmprop2.p1.m5" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\sigma" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>σ</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\sigma</annotation></semantics></math>. Then, at every iteration, GASP with Absolute Maximum linkage (or, in other words, the Mutex Watershed algorithm) always makes a mistake with at least probability <math id="S6.Thmprop2.p1.m6" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\eta" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>η</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\eta</annotation></semantics></math>.</span></p>
</div>
</div>
<div id="S6.SS4.2" class="ltx_proof">
<h6 class="ltx_title ltx_runin ltx_font_italic ltx_title_proof">Proof.</h6>
<div id="S6.SS4.p1" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S6.SS4.p1.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="S6.SS4.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text">Thanks to Lemma <a href="#S6.Thmtheorem3" title="Lemma A6.3. ‣ A6.3 Proofs of Propositions , , , and ‣ A6 Appendix ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">A6.3</span></a> we know that GASP with Absolute Maximum linkage returns the same clustering whether or not cannot-link-constraints are used. Thus, in the following, we prove the proposition considering the version enforcing constraints.
Let us consider a generic iteration of the algorithm, where two clusters <math id="S6.SS4.p1.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="S_{\alpha}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msub><mi>S</mi><mi>α</mi></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">S_{\alpha}</annotation></semantics></math> and <math id="S6.SS4.p1.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="S_{\beta}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msub><mi>S</mi><mi>β</mi></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">S_{\beta}</annotation></semantics></math> have the highest priority and are popped from priority queue. Then, the MWS algorithm will either merge or constrain them depending on the fact that their interaction <math id="S6.SS4.p1.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{AbsMax}}(S_{\alpha}\cup S_{\beta})" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><msub><mi class="ltx_font_mathcaligraphic">𝒲</mi><mi>AbsMax</mi></msub><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><msub><mi>S</mi><mi>α</mi></msub><mo>∪</mo><msub><mi>S</mi><mi>β</mi></msub></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{AbsMax}}(S_{\alpha}\cup S_{\beta})</annotation></semantics></math> is positive or negative (note that, with AbsMax linkage, an interaction can never be positive and constrained, as shown in Lemma <a href="#S6.Thmtheorem3" title="Lemma A6.3. ‣ A6.3 Proofs of Propositions , , , and ‣ A6 Appendix ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">A6.3</span></a>).
By construction of the SSBM, every edge <math id="S6.SS4.p1.m4" class="ltx_Math" alttext="e\in E" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>e</mi><mo>∈</mo><mi>E</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">e\in E</annotation></semantics></math> in the graph has a absolute weight distributed as <math id="S6.SS4.p1.m5" class="ltx_Math" alttext="|w_{e}|\sim\mathcal{N}(1,\sigma^{2})" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">|</mo><msub><mi>w</mi><mi>e</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">|</mo></mrow><mo>∼</mo><mrow><mi class="ltx_font_mathcaligraphic">𝒩</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>,</mo><msup><mi>σ</mi><mn>2</mn></msup><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|w_{e}|\sim\mathcal{N}(1,\sigma^{2})</annotation></semantics></math>. Thus, every edge <math id="S6.SS4.p1.m6" class="ltx_Math" alttext="e^{\prime}\in(S_{\alpha}\times S_{\beta})\cap E" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><msup><mi>e</mi><mo>′</mo></msup><mo>∈</mo><mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><msub><mi>S</mi><mi>α</mi></msub><mo lspace="0.222em" rspace="0.222em">×</mo><msub><mi>S</mi><mi>β</mi></msub></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo>∩</mo><mi>E</mi></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">e^{\prime}\in(S_{\alpha}\times S_{\beta})\cap E</annotation></semantics></math> connecting the two clusters has the same probability to have the highest absolute weight, and the sign of the interaction <math id="S6.SS4.p1.m7" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{AbsMax}}(S_{\alpha}\cup S_{\beta})" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><msub><mi class="ltx_font_mathcaligraphic">𝒲</mi><mi>AbsMax</mi></msub><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><msub><mi>S</mi><mi>α</mi></msub><mo>∪</mo><msub><mi>S</mi><mi>β</mi></msub></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{AbsMax}}(S_{\alpha}\cup S_{\beta})</annotation></semantics></math> will only depend on the sign of this highest edge. Therefore, the probability that the MWS merges two clusters is simply given by the fraction of positive weighted edges connecting them.</span></p>
</div>
<div id="S6.SS4.p2" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S6.SS4.p2.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="S6.SS4.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text">Let <math id="S6.SS4.p2.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\tilde{\Pi}=\{\tilde{S}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{S}_{k}\}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mover accent="true"><mi mathvariant="normal">Π</mi><mo>~</mo></mover><mo>=</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">{</mo><msub><mover accent="true"><mi>S</mi><mo>~</mo></mover><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">…</mi><mo>,</mo><msub><mover accent="true"><mi>S</mi><mo>~</mo></mover><mi>k</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">}</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\tilde{\Pi}=\{\tilde{S}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{S}_{k}\}</annotation></semantics></math> denote the ground truth clustering, and <math id="S6.SS4.p2.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\tilde{S}_{\alpha i}=S_{\alpha}\cap\tilde{S}_{i}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><msub><mover accent="true"><mi>S</mi><mo>~</mo></mover><mrow><mi>α</mi><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>i</mi></mrow></msub><mo>=</mo><mrow><msub><mi>S</mi><mi>α</mi></msub><mo>∩</mo><msub><mover accent="true"><mi>S</mi><mo>~</mo></mover><mi>i</mi></msub></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\tilde{S}_{\alpha i}=S_{\alpha}\cap\tilde{S}_{i}</annotation></semantics></math> denote the intersection between cluster <math id="S6.SS4.p2.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="S_{\alpha}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msub><mi>S</mi><mi>α</mi></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">S_{\alpha}</annotation></semantics></math> and a ground-truth cluster <math id="S6.SS4.p2.m4" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\tilde{S}_{i}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msub><mover accent="true"><mi>S</mi><mo>~</mo></mover><mi>i</mi></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\tilde{S}_{i}</annotation></semantics></math>.
If the generated graph is dense, i.e. <math id="S6.SS4.p2.m5" class="ltx_Math" alttext="p=1" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>p</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">p=1</annotation></semantics></math>, then the total number of edges connecting clusters <math id="S6.SS4.p2.m6" class="ltx_Math" alttext="S_{\alpha}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msub><mi>S</mi><mi>α</mi></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">S_{\alpha}</annotation></semantics></math> and <math id="S6.SS4.p2.m7" class="ltx_Math" alttext="S_{\beta}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msub><mi>S</mi><mi>β</mi></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">S_{\beta}</annotation></semantics></math> that have a true attractive or repulsive weight is (according to the ground truth labels)</span></p>
<table id="S6.E21" class="ltx_equation ltx_eqn_table">

<tbody><tr class="ltx_equation ltx_eqn_row ltx_align_baseline">

</table>
<p id="S6.SS4.p2.2" class="ltx_p"><span id="S6.SS4.p2.2.1" class="ltx_text">When the edges in the graph are randomly added with a probability <math id="S6.SS4.p2.m8" class="ltx_Math" alttext="p" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>p</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">p</annotation></semantics></math>, then the actual number of true attractive and repulsive interactions connecting the two clusters is (according to the ground truth labels):</span></p>

</table>
<p id="S6.SS4.p2.3" class="ltx_p"><span id="S6.SS4.p2.3.1" class="ltx_text">where <math id="S6.SS4.p2.m9" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathcal{B}(\Gamma,p)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi class="ltx_font_mathcaligraphic">ℬ</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">Γ</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>p</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathcal{B}(\Gamma,p)</annotation></semantics></math> is the binomial distribution:</span></p>
<table id="S6.E23" class="ltx_equation ltx_eqn_table">

<td class="ltx_eqn_cell ltx_align_center"><math id="S6.E23.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathcal{B}(\gamma;\Gamma,p)=\frac{\Gamma!}{\gamma!(\Gamma-\gamma)!}p^{\gamma}(1-p)^{\Gamma-\gamma}." display="block" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mrow><mrow><mi class="ltx_font_mathcaligraphic">ℬ</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>γ</mi><mo>,</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">Γ</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>p</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>=</mo><mrow><mfrac><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">Γ</mi><mo>!</mo></mrow><mrow><mrow><mrow><mi>γ</mi><mo>!</mo></mrow><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">Γ</mi><mo>−</mo><mi>γ</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>!</mo></mrow></mfrac><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><msup><mi>p</mi><mi>γ</mi></msup><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><msup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>−</mo><mi>p</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">Γ</mi><mo>−</mo><mi>γ</mi></mrow></msup></mrow></mrow><mo lspace="0em">.</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathcal{B}(\gamma;\Gamma,p)=\frac{\Gamma!}{\gamma!(\Gamma-\gamma)!}p^{\gamma}(1-p)^{\Gamma-\gamma}.</annotation></semantics></math></td>
<td class="ltx_eqn_cell ltx_eqn_center_padright"></td>
<td rowspan="1" class="ltx_eqn_cell ltx_eqn_eqno ltx_align_middle ltx_align_right"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_equation ltx_align_right">(23)</span></td></tr></tbody>
</table>
<p id="S6.SS4.p2.4" class="ltx_p"><span id="S6.SS4.p2.4.1" class="ltx_text">Here, we only assume that <math id="S6.SS4.p2.m10" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\gamma^{+}+\gamma^{-}&gt;0" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mrow><msup><mi>γ</mi><mo>+</mo></msup><mo>+</mo><msup><mi>γ</mi><mo>−</mo></msup></mrow><mo>&gt;</mo><mn>0</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\gamma^{+}+\gamma^{-}&gt;0</annotation></semantics></math>, i.e. there is at least one edge connecting the two clusters (otherwise their interaction would be zero and the MWS would not have popped them from priority queue).</span></p>
</div>
<div id="S6.SS4.p3" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S6.SS4.p3.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="S6.SS4.p3.1.1" class="ltx_text">So far we have been talking about attractive and repulsive connections according to the ground truth labels. In our SSBM however every edge has a uniform probability <math id="S6.SS4.p3.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\eta" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>η</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\eta</annotation></semantics></math> to have its sign flip, so the actual number of attractive interactions connecting the two clusters will be instead given by the sum of the true attractive interactions <math id="S6.SS4.p3.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\gamma^{+}_{\mathrm{nf}}\sim\mathcal{B}(\gamma^{+},1-\eta)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><msubsup><mi>γ</mi><mi>nf</mi><mo>+</mo></msubsup><mo>∼</mo><mrow><mi class="ltx_font_mathcaligraphic">ℬ</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msup><mi>γ</mi><mo>+</mo></msup><mo>,</mo><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>−</mo><mi>η</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\gamma^{+}_{\mathrm{nf}}\sim\mathcal{B}(\gamma^{+},1-\eta)</annotation></semantics></math> that have not been flipped, plus the true negative interactions <math id="S6.SS4.p3.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\gamma^{-}_{\mathrm{f}}\sim\mathcal{B}(\gamma^{-},\eta)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><msubsup><mi>γ</mi><mi>f</mi><mo>−</mo></msubsup><mo>∼</mo><mrow><mi class="ltx_font_mathcaligraphic">ℬ</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msup><mi>γ</mi><mo>−</mo></msup><mo>,</mo><mi>η</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\gamma^{-}_{\mathrm{f}}\sim\mathcal{B}(\gamma^{-},\eta)</annotation></semantics></math> that have been flipped.
Putting everything together, given two clusters with <math id="S6.SS4.p3.m4" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\gamma^{+}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msup><mi>γ</mi><mo>+</mo></msup><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\gamma^{+}</annotation></semantics></math> true attractive interactions and <math id="S6.SS4.p3.m5" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\gamma^{-}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msup><mi>γ</mi><mo>−</mo></msup><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\gamma^{-}</annotation></semantics></math> true negative ones, the highest-absolute-weight edge connecting them has the following probability to be positive:</span></p>

<td class="ltx_eqn_cell ltx_eqn_center_padleft"></td>
<td class="ltx_td ltx_align_right ltx_eqn_cell"><math id="S6.E24.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(*)}}{{=}}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mover><mo>=</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">∗</mo><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mover><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(*)}}{{=}}</annotation></semantics></math></td>
<td class="ltx_td ltx_align_left ltx_eqn_cell"><math id="S6.E24.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\displaystyle\frac{\gamma^{+}(1-\eta)+\gamma^{-}\eta}{\gamma^{+}+\gamma^{-}}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mstyle displaystyle="true"><mfrac><mrow><mrow><msup><mi>γ</mi><mo>+</mo></msup><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>−</mo><mi>η</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>+</mo><mrow><msup><mi>γ</mi><mo>−</mo></msup><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>η</mi></mrow></mrow><mrow><msup><mi>γ</mi><mo>+</mo></msup><mo>+</mo><msup><mi>γ</mi><mo>−</mo></msup></mrow></mfrac></mstyle><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\displaystyle\frac{\gamma^{+}(1-\eta)+\gamma^{-}\eta}{\gamma^{+}+\gamma^{-}}</annotation></semantics></math></td>
<td class="ltx_eqn_cell ltx_eqn_center_padright"></td>
<td rowspan="1" class="ltx_eqn_cell ltx_eqn_eqno ltx_align_middle ltx_align_right"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_equation ltx_align_right">(24)</span></td></tr></tbody>
</table>
<p id="S6.SS4.p3.2" class="ltx_p"><span id="S6.SS4.p3.2.1" class="ltx_text">where in <math id="S6.SS4.p3.m6" class="ltx_Math" alttext="(*)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">∗</mo><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">(*)</annotation></semantics></math> we used the fact that the expected value of a binomial distribution <math id="S6.SS4.p3.m7" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathcal{B}(\gamma,\eta)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi class="ltx_font_mathcaligraphic">ℬ</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>γ</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>η</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathcal{B}(\gamma,\eta)</annotation></semantics></math> is <math id="S6.SS4.p3.m8" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\gamma\eta" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>γ</mi><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>η</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\gamma\eta</annotation></semantics></math>.</span></p>
</div>
<div id="S6.SS4.p4" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S6.SS4.p4.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="S6.SS4.p4.1.1" class="ltx_text">Now we note that this probability is bounded in the interval <math id="S6.SS4.p4.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="[\eta,1-\eta]" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mrow><mi>η</mi><mo>,</mo><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>−</mo><mi>η</mi></mrow></mrow><mo stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">[\eta,1-\eta]</annotation></semantics></math>. So, regardless of whether the two clusters <math id="S6.SS4.p4.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="S_{\alpha}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msub><mi>S</mi><mi>α</mi></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">S_{\alpha}</annotation></semantics></math> and <math id="S6.SS4.p4.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="S_{\beta}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msub><mi>S</mi><mi>β</mi></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">S_{\beta}</annotation></semantics></math> should be merged or constraint according to ground truth labels, the probability not to make the correct decision is always at least <math id="S6.SS4.p4.m4" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\eta" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>η</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\eta</annotation></semantics></math>.
Remarkably, while the exact probability in Eq. <a href="#S6.Ex3" title="Proof. ‣ A6.4 Mutex Watershed on SSBM graphs ‣ A6 Appendix ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">A6.4</span></a> depends on the number of edges connecting the two clusters <math id="S6.SS4.p4.m5" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\gamma^{+}+\gamma^{-}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><msup><mi>γ</mi><mo>+</mo></msup><mo>+</mo><msup><mi>γ</mi><mo>−</mo></msup></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\gamma^{+}+\gamma^{-}</annotation></semantics></math> and thus on the cluster sizes, the bounds do not. Thus, this result shows that, unlike Sum or Avg linkage methods, the MWS algorithm is unable to reliably correct for the sign flip noise even for big clusters linked by many edges.
∎</span></p>
</div>
</div>
</section>
<section id="S6.SS5" class="ltx_subsection">
<h3 class="ltx_title ltx_title_subsection"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">A6.5 </span>Application to neuron segmentation</h3>

<section id="S6.SS5.SSS0.Px1" class="ltx_paragraph">
<h4 class="ltx_title ltx_title_paragraph">Training and data augmentation</h4>

<div id="S6.SS5.SSS0.Px1.p1" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S6.SS5.SSS0.Px1.p1.1" class="ltx_p">The data from the CREMI challenge is highly anisotropic and contains artifacts like missing sections, staining precipitations and support film folds.
To alleviate difficulties stemming from misalignment, we use a version of the data that was elastically realigned by the challenge organizers with the method of <em id="S6.SS5.SSS0.Px1.p1.1.1" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">S. Saalfeld, et al. Nature methods, 2012</em>.
In addition to the standard data augmentation techniques of random rotations, random flips and elastic deformations, we simulate data artifacts.
We randomly zero-out slices, decrease the contrast of slices, simulate tears, introduce alignment jitter and paste artifacts extracted from the training data. Both <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite"><a href="#bib.bib28" title="" class="ltx_ref">funke2018large</a></cite> and <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite"><a href="#bib.bib50" title="" class="ltx_ref">lee2017superhuman</a></cite> have shown
that these kinds of augmentations can help to alleviate issues caused by EM-imaging artifacts.
We use L2 loss and Adam optimizer to train the network. The model was trained on all three samples with available ground truth labels.</p>
</div>
</section>
<section id="S6.SS5.SSS0.Px2" class="ltx_paragraph">
<h4 class="ltx_title ltx_title_paragraph">CREMI-gridRag instances</h4>

<div id="S6.SS5.SSS0.Px2.p1" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S6.SS5.SSS0.Px2.p1.1" class="ltx_p">Our 3D UNet model predicts the same set of 12 long-and-short range affinities as described in <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite"><a href="#bib.bib50" title="" class="ltx_ref">lee2017superhuman</a></cite>.
When building the pixel-grid graph, we add both direct neighbors connections and the long-range connections predicted by our model (every voxel is connected to other six voxels via direct connections and other 18 voxels via long-range edges).
Empirically, when long-range predictions of the CNN are added as long-range connections in the graph, GASP achieves better scores as compared to when only direct-neighbors predictions are used.
Our intuitive explanation of this is that, where there is a clear boundary evidence between two segments, the long-range predictions of the CNN model are more certain than the direct-neighbor ones, because it is often impossible to estimate the exact ground-truth label transition for pixels that are very close to a boundary evidence.
However, empirically, we also find that GASP achieves the best scores when only 10% of the long-range connections are randomly sampled and added to the grid-graph. When all the long-range connections predicted by the CNN are added to the graph (18 connections for every voxel), all versions of GASP tend to perform more over-clustering errors.
In practice, we explain this by observing that many challenging parts of the studied neuron segmentation data involve thin and elongated segments, and our model sometimes fails to connect distant pairs of pixels that, according to the ground-truth labels, should belong to the same segment (even though, in this case, the direct neighboring predictions are correct).
To sum up, the scores we report in Tables <a href="#S4.T3.st1" title="Table 3a) ‣ Table 3 ‣ 4.1 Signed graph clustering problems ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3a)</span></a> are obtained by using only 10% of the long-range predictions, since this was the setup that performed the best.
After running GASP, we use a simple post-processing step to delete small segments on the boundaries, most of which are given by single-voxel clusters. On the neuron segmentation predictions, we deleted all regions with less than 200 voxels and used a seeded watershed algorithm to expand the bigger segments.</p>
</div>
</section>
<section id="S6.SS5.SSS0.Px3" class="ltx_paragraph">
<h4 class="ltx_title ltx_title_paragraph">CREMI-3D-rag instances</h4>

<div id="S6.SS5.SSS0.Px3.p1" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S6.SS5.SSS0.Px3.p1.1" class="ltx_p">We build these clustering problems by generating superpixels and then building a 3D region adjacency graph.
Due to the anisotropy of the data, we generate 2D superpixels by considering each 2D image in the stack singularly.
First, we generate a boundary-evidence map by taking an average over the two direct-neighbor predictions of the CNN model (one for each direction in the 2D image of the stack) and applying some additional smoothing. Then, we threshold the boundary map, compute a distance transform, and run a watershed algorithm seeded at the maxima of the distance transform (WSDT). The degree of smoothing was optimized such that each region receives as few seeds as possible, without however causing severe under-segmentation.
The computed 2D superpixels are then used to build a 3D region-adjacency graph (3D-rag). The weights of the edges are given by averaging the CNN affinities over the boundaries of adjacent superpixels.</p>
</div>
<figure id="S6.T5" class="ltx_table ltx_align_center">
<figcaption class="ltx_caption ltx_centering" style="font-size:70%;"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_table"><span id="S6.T5.9" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:129%;">Table A5</span>: </span><span id="S6.T5.10" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:129%;">We compare algorithms in the GASP framework by evaluating which of the obtained clusterings is associated to the lowest value of the multicut objective defined in Eq. <a href="#S3.E1" title="Equation 1 ‣ 3.1 Notation ‣ 3 Generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a> (lower is better). Single and complete linkage methods performed much worse than the others. Note that HCC-Single is the algorithm with the highest runtime (see Table <a href="#S4.T3.st1" title="Table 3a) ‣ Table 3 ‣ 4.1 Signed graph clustering problems ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3a)</span></a>) and it did not scale up to the very large clustering problem <em id="S6.T5.10.1" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">Fruit-Fly Level Global</em>.
</span></figcaption>

<td id="S6.T5.11.6.8" class="ltx_td ltx_align_right"><span id="S6.T5.11.6.8.1" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:70%;">-71,997</span></td>

<tr id="S6.T5.11.7" class="ltx_tr">

</section>
</section>
<section id="S6.SS6" class="ltx_subsection">
<h3 class="ltx_title ltx_title_subsection"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">A6.6 </span>Adding structured noise to CNN predictions</h3>

<div id="S6.SS6.p1" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S6.SS6.p1.1" class="ltx_p">Additionally to the comparison on the full training dataset, we performed more experiments on a crop of the more challenging CREMI training sample B, where we perturbed the predictions of the CNN with noise and we introduced additional artifacts like missing boundary evidences.</p>
</div>
<div id="S6.SS6.p2" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S6.SS6.p2.1" class="ltx_p">In the field of image processing there are several ways of adding noise to an image, among which the most common are Gaussian noise or Poisson shot noise.
In these cases, the noise of one pixel does not correlate with its neighboring noise values. On the other hand, predictions of a CNN are known to be spatially correlated.
Thus, we used Perlin noise<span id="footnote14" class="ltx_note ltx_role_footnote"><sup class="ltx_note_mark">14</sup><span class="ltx_note_outer"><span class="ltx_note_content"><sup class="ltx_note_mark">14</sup>
              <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_note">14</span>
              
              
              
              
              
              
              
            In our experiments, we used an open-source implementation of simplex noise <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite"><a href="#bib.bib64" title="" class="ltx_ref">perlin2001noise</a></cite>, which is an improved version of Perlin noise <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite"><a href="#bib.bib63" title="" class="ltx_ref">perlin1985image</a></cite></span></span></span>, one of the most common gradient noises used in procedural pattern generation. This type of noise <math id="S6.SS6.p2.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="n(x)\in[0,1]" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mrow><mi>n</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>∈</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mrow><mn>0</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mo stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">n(x)\in[0,1]</annotation></semantics></math> generates spatial random patterns that are locally smooth but have large and diverse variations on bigger scales. We then combined it with the CNN predictions <math id="S6.SS6.p2.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="p(x)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>p</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">p(x)</annotation></semantics></math> in the following way:</p>
<table id="S6.E25" class="ltx_equation ltx_eqn_table">

<tbody><tr class="ltx_equation ltx_eqn_row ltx_align_baseline">
<td class="ltx_eqn_cell ltx_eqn_center_padleft"></td>
<td class="ltx_eqn_cell ltx_align_center"><math id="S6.E25.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\tilde{F}(x;\mathcal{K})=F(x)+\mathcal{K}\cdot\max\left(N(x),0\right)," display="block" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mrow><mrow><mover accent="true"><mi>F</mi><mo>~</mo></mover><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo>,</mo><mi class="ltx_font_mathcaligraphic">𝒦</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>=</mo><mrow><mrow><mi>F</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>+</mo><mrow><mi class="ltx_font_mathcaligraphic">𝒦</mi><mo lspace="0.222em" rspace="0.222em">⋅</mo><mrow><mi>max</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo>(</mo><mrow><mi>N</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>,</mo><mn>0</mn><mo>)</mo></mrow></mrow></mrow></mrow></mrow><mo>,</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\tilde{F}(x;\mathcal{K})=F(x)+\mathcal{K}\cdot\max\left(N(x),0\right),</annotation></semantics></math></td>
<td class="ltx_eqn_cell ltx_eqn_center_padright"></td>
<td rowspan="1" class="ltx_eqn_cell ltx_eqn_eqno ltx_align_middle ltx_align_right"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_equation ltx_align_right">(25)</span></td></tr></tbody>
</table>
<p id="S6.SS6.p2.2" class="ltx_p">where <math id="S6.SS6.p2.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="N(x)=\mathrm{Logit}[n(x)]" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mrow><mi>N</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>=</mo><mrow><mi>Logit</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mrow><mi>n</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">N(x)=\mathrm{Logit}[n(x)]</annotation></semantics></math>; <math id="S6.SS6.p2.m4" class="ltx_Math" alttext="F(x)=\mathrm{Logit}[p(x)]" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mrow><mi>F</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>=</mo><mrow><mi>Logit</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mrow><mi>p</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">F(x)=\mathrm{Logit}[p(x)]</annotation></semantics></math> and <math id="S6.SS6.p2.m5" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathcal{K}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi class="ltx_font_mathcaligraphic">𝒦</mi><mo>∈</mo><msup><mi>ℝ</mi><mo>+</mo></msup></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathcal{K}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}</annotation></semantics></math> is a positive factor representing the amount of added noise. The resulting perturbed predictions <math id="S6.SS6.p2.m6" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\tilde{F}(x;\mathcal{K})" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mover accent="true"><mi>F</mi><mo>~</mo></mover><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo>,</mo><mi class="ltx_font_mathcaligraphic">𝒦</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\tilde{F}(x;\mathcal{K})</annotation></semantics></math> are then under-clustering biased, such that the probability for two pixels to be in the same cluster is increased only if <math id="S6.SS6.p2.m7" class="ltx_Math" alttext="N(x)&gt;0" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mrow><mi>N</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>&gt;</mo><mn>0</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">N(x)&gt;0</annotation></semantics></math> (see Fig. <a href="#S6.F11" title="Figure A11 ‣ A6.6 Adding structured noise to CNN predictions ‣ A6 Appendix ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_ref ltx_nolink"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">A11</span></span>b</a> and <a href="#S6.F11" title="Figure A11 ‣ A6.6 Adding structured noise to CNN predictions ‣ A6 Appendix ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_ref ltx_nolink"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">A11</span></span>c</a>).
Note that in these experiments we focused only on predictions perturbed with under-clustering biased noise (and not over-clustering biased noise). The reason is that generating realistic over-clustering biased CNN predictions is more complex and cannot be simply done by adding Perlin noise: as we show in Fig. <a href="#S6.F11" title="Figure A11 ‣ A6.6 Adding structured noise to CNN predictions ‣ A6 Appendix ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_ref ltx_nolink"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">A11</span></span>c</a>, by adding Perlin noise we can easily “remove” parts of a boundary evidence, but it is not possible to generate random new realistic boundary evidence.</p>
</div>
<div id="S6.SS6.p3" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S6.SS6.p3.1" class="ltx_p">In our experiments, each pixel is represented by a node in the grid-graph and it is linked to <math id="S6.SS6.p3.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="n_{\mathrm{nb}}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msub><mi>n</mi><mi>nb</mi></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">n_{\mathrm{nb}}</annotation></semantics></math> other nodes by short- and long-range edges. Thus, the output volume of our CNN model is a four-dimensional tensor with <math id="S6.SS6.p3.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="n_{\mathrm{nb}}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msub><mi>n</mi><mi>nb</mi></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">n_{\mathrm{nb}}</annotation></semantics></math> channels: for each pixel / voxel, the model outputs <math id="S6.SS6.p3.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="n_{\mathrm{nb}}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msub><mi>n</mi><mi>nb</mi></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">n_{\mathrm{nb}}</annotation></semantics></math> values representing affinities of different edge connections. We then generated a 4-dimensional Perlin noise tensor that matches the dimension of the CNN output. The data is highly anisotropic, i.e. it has a lower resolution in one of the dimensions. Due to this fact, we chose different smoothing parameters to generate the noise in different directions.</p>
</div>
<figure id="S6.F11" class="ltx_figure"><img src="/html/1906.11713/assets/noisy_affs_comparison_v2.png" id="S6.F11.g1" class="ltx_graphics ltx_centering ltx_img_landscape" width="685" height="279" alt="Refer to caption">
<figcaption class="ltx_caption ltx_centering"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure"><span id="S6.F11.6" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">Figure A11</span>: </span><span id="S6.F11.7" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">CNN predictions on a slice of the CREMI neuron segmentation challenge with and without additional spatially-correlated noise. (<span id="S6.F11.7.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">a</span>) Raw data (<span id="S6.F11.7.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">b</span>) Original CNN predictions <math id="S6.F11.m4" class="ltx_Math" alttext="F(x)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>F</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">F(x)</annotation></semantics></math>, where blue pixels represent boundary evidence (<span id="S6.F11.7.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">c</span>) Strongly perturbed version <math id="S6.F11.m5" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\tilde{F}(x;\mathcal{K})" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mover accent="true"><mi>F</mi><mo mathsize="1.110em">~</mo></mover><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo>,</mo><mi class="ltx_font_mathcaligraphic">𝒦</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\tilde{F}(x;\mathcal{K})</annotation></semantics></math> of the predictions defined in Eq. <a href="#S6.E25" title="Equation 25 ‣ A6.6 Adding structured noise to CNN predictions ‣ A6 Appendix ‣ GASP, a generalized framework for agglomerative clustering of signed graphs and its application to Instance Segmentation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">25</span></a> with <math id="S6.F11.m6" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathcal{K}=8" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi class="ltx_font_mathcaligraphic">𝒦</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>8</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathcal{K}=8</annotation></semantics></math>. Long-range predictions are not shown.
</span></figcaption>
</figure>

      var canMathML = typeof(MathMLElement) == "function";
      if (!canMathML) {
        var body = document.querySelector("body");
        body.firstElementChild.setAttribute('style', 'opacity: 0;');
        var loading = document.createElement("div");
        loading.setAttribute("id", "mathjax-loading-spinner");
        var message = document.createElement("div");
        message.setAttribute("id", "mathjax-loading-message");
        message.innerText = "Typesetting Equations...";
        body.prepend(loading);
        body.prepend(message);

        var el = document.createElement("script");
        el.src = "https://cdn.jsdelivr.net/npm/mathjax@3/es5/tex-mml-chtml.js";
        document.querySelector("head").appendChild(el);

        window.MathJax = {
          startup: {
            pageReady: () => {
              return MathJax.startup.defaultPageReady().then(() => {
                body.removeChild(loading);
                body.removeChild(message);
                body.firstElementChild.removeAttribute('style');
              }); } } };
      }
    </script>
    <script>
    // Auxiliary function, building the preview feature when
    // an inline citation is clicked
    function clicked_cite(e) {
      e.preventDefault();
      let cite = this.closest('.ltx_cite');
      let next = cite.nextSibling;
      if (next && next.nodeType == Node.ELEMENT_NODE && next.getAttribute('class') == "ar5iv-bibitem-preview") {
        next.remove();
        return; }
      // Before adding a preview modal,
      // cleanup older previews, in case they're still open
      document.querySelectorAll('span.ar5iv-bibitem-preview').forEach(function(node) {
        node.remove();
      })

      // Create the preview
      preview = document.createElement('span');
      preview.setAttribute('class','ar5iv-bibitem-preview');
      let target = document.getElementById(this.getAttribute('href').slice(1));
      target.childNodes.forEach(function (child) {
        preview.append(child.cloneNode(true));
      });
      let close_x = document.createElement('button');
      close_x.setAttribute("aria-label","Close modal for bibliography item preview");
      close_x.textContent = "×";
      close_x.setAttribute('class', 'ar5iv-button-close-preview');
      close_x.setAttribute('onclick','this.parentNode.remove()');
      preview.append(close_x);
      preview.querySelectorAll('.ltx_tag_bibitem').forEach(function(node) {
        node.remove();
      });
      cite.parentNode.insertBefore(preview, cite.nextSibling);
      return;
    }
    // Global Document initialization:
    // - assign the preview feature to all inline citation links
    document.querySelectorAll(".ltx_cite .ltx_ref").forEach(function (link) {
      link.addEventListener("click", clicked_cite);
    });
    </script>
